Regarding This Volume

Volume 7 is a record of uprisings, protests, regime overthrows, civil wars, and coups in 35 countries of the Americas. The regional composition and country order follow the Country Analysis Series (2026-10-07). The method of description, dataset definitions, rules for linking countries, and axioms and determinants are described in Volume 1.

The numbers in the table below represent the number of records linked to this country in each dataset, as counted by the program from the raw data. A value of 0 indicates that there are no records in that dataset, and the magnitude of the numbers varies according to the definitions, thresholds, and reporting scope of the datasets (Volume 1, Part 4).

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Mixtón War Early colonial period · 1541–1542

Chichimeca indigenous peoples in Nueva Galicia (northwestern Mexico) rose up against Spanish colonial rule. According to the Encyclopedia.com (Latin American History & Culture Encyclopedia) account, background factors included the harsh rule of Nuño de Guzmán, subsequent mistreatment by encomenderos, and resentment over imposed foreign customs; religious leaders led the uprising, promising divine favor and rewards. Pedro de Alvarado, who initially attempted to suppress the uprising, died from a fall from his horse, and Antonio de Mendoza, the viceroy, suppressed it with about 180 cavalrymen and thousands of indigenous allies from central Mexico. The pacification of Nueva Galicia led to the discovery of silver mines in Zacatecas in 1546. The start year of the uprising is given as either 1540 or 1541 depending on the source.

  1. Mixtón War - Encyclopedia of Latin American History and Culture (Encyclopedia.com) · Encyclopedia — https://www.encyclopedia.com/humanities/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/mixton-war

Mexico City Corn Riot (1692 Tultitlán) Mid-colonial period · 1692

Following heavy rains and floods in 1691, crop pests and poor harvests ensued, driving maize prices to a century-high and tripling food prices, but price controls were not imposed. When rumors spread that managers at the granary (alondiga) had suffocated an impoverished indigenous woman, crowds gathered outside the Archbishop's Palace on June 8, 1692, moved on to the Viceroy's Palace, were turned away, and a riot broke out in the evening. The crowd swelled to thousands, including lower-class whites; they burned the gates of the Viceroy's Palace, his carriage, the city hall building, the archives, and the granary, and looted nearly 300 shops and street vendors. Armed Spaniards returned to the plaza and attacked the remaining rioters, killing dozens and wounding several times that number. About 60 people were sentenced to punishments ranging from flogging to public humiliation, and damages totaled approximately 2 million pesos. The authorities subsequently strengthened the military and imposed new restrictions on indigenous people. The first major uprising of the century occurred in 1624 (Cebu could not confirm this).

  1. Mexico City Corn Riots - EBSCO Research Starters · Academic Overview — https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/ethnic-and-cultural-studies/mexico-city-corn-riots

Chetel Rebellion (Canul Rebellion) Late Colonial Period · 1712

In early summer 1712, young Maya women in Canul, a highland Chiapas village, reported seeing an apparition of the Virgin in the forest; indigenous pilgrims from nearby villages gathered. When Spanish authorities sought to suppress the belief, it erupted into full-scale rebellion, and the rebel leadership assembled about 5,000 'soldiers of the Virgin' and declared that 'there is no king or priest now.' Kevin Gosner (Gosner 1992) interprets this uprising as a 'moral economy' defense against the clash between late-17th-century colonial administrative reforms and new economic demands and Maya norms regarding the ceremonial obligations and reciprocal politics of community leaders (author's interpretation). Trial records, letters by Dominican priests, bishops, and Spanish commanders are the primary sources.

  1. Kevin Gosner, Soldiers of the Virgin (University of Arizona Press, 1992) Introduction · Academic Monograph · Orientation: Author's Interpretation (Moral Economy Theory) — https://uapress.arizona.edu/book/soldiers-of-the-virgin

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Mexican Revolution Civil War · 1910–1920

A revolution that began with a rebellion against Porfirio Díaz's long rule in 1910 and turned into a civil war among multiple armed factions. Population cost estimates vary widely among researchers. The 'Missing Millions' table of the University of Minnesota Population Center (McCaa) compares nine estimates, including those of Camio, Rojo, González Navarro, Coulburn, Greer, Mier y Terán, and Ordoñez y Lázaro, and presents total population loss due to emigration, reduced births, and census error at approximately 1.9 million to 3.4 million. This value represents the total population loss, not just deaths.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Total Demographic Loss (Deaths + Emigration + Reduced Births + Census Error)Mexican government's official single tally could not be confirmedApprox. 1.9 million (González Navarro) to 3.4 million (Ordoñez y Lázaro, Reséndiz) — McCaa comparison table

Data Record: The COW civil war data records this period as the 'Third Mexican War of 1910-1914' and the 'Fourth Mexican War of 1914-1920' (see table). Because COW counts only combat deaths, it cannot be directly compared with demographic loss estimates.

  1. Missing Millions: The human cost of the Mexican Revolution, 1910-1921 (University of Minnesota Population Center, McCaa) · Academic Data — https://users.pop.umn.edu/~rmccaa/missmill/mxrevtbl.htm

Tlatelolco Massacre (1968 Student Movement) Protest · 1968

On October 2, 1968, shots were fired at a student movement rally in the Plaza de las Tres Culturas in Tlatelolco, Mexico City. U.S. government documents released by the National Security Archive describe that when the shooting stopped, 'hundreds lay dead or wounded.' A CIA cable dated October 3 reported 24 civilian deaths and 137 injuries, but the archive's release notes that this report underestimated the death toll. The final death toll has not been confirmed.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsThe Mexican government's official figures are not present on the pages reviewed for this entryCIA cable 1968.10.3: deaths 24, wounded 137 (archive notes underestimation) / Archive release: 'hundreds killed'

Data Record: As a single-day suppression event, it does not meet the criteria of COW or UCDP armed conflict. Because protest data (MM) cover only from 1990 onward, this event is not included.

  1. Kate Doyle (ed.), The Tlatelolco Massacre: U.S. Documents on Mexico and the Events of 1968 — National Security Archive Electronic Briefing Book No. 99 · Record Collection · Orientation: U.S. government documents (external observer standpoint) — https://nsarchive2.gwu.edu/NSAEBB/NSAEBB99/

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Bacon's Rebellion Colonial Period (British Colony) · 1676

In Virginia, falling tobacco prices, competition with Maryland and Carolina, British trade restrictions and high commodity prices, war with the Dutch, and poor weather coincided in 1675, when Nathaniel Bacon led a force opposing Governor Berkeley's policies and taxes following clashes with Native Americans. On September 19, 1676, Bacon burned Jamestown, fortified by the governor's forces (many historical records state this), but he died suddenly on October 26 of that year from dysentery and other causes. Berkeley subsequently regained control, hanged 23 participants, and confiscated their property without trial, while Britain removed Berkeley from the governorship (National Park Service account).

  1. Bacon's Rebellion - Historic Jamestowne (U.S. National Park Service) · Government Public History — https://www.nps.gov/jame/learn/historyculture/bacons-rebellion.htm

Pueblo Revolt Colonial Period (Spanish Nueva España) · 1680

On August 10, 1680, Pueblo Indians under the leadership of Po'Pay (Oke Owinge, San Juan Pueblo) launched coordinated attacks against Spanish colonial authorities and the missionary system. The Spanish were expelled from Santa Fe, and they remained out of the region for about 12 years until Diego de Vargas recaptured Santa Fe in 1692 (Library of Congress Law Library description). This event is included in the U.S. volume of this series as it occurred on U.S. territory. The number of fatalities is not recorded in the consulted sources.

  1. 1680: The Pueblo Revolt - In Custodia Legis (Law Library of Congress) · Government Public History — https://blogs.loc.gov/law/2013/10/1680-the-pueblo-revolt/

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Shays's Rebellion 1786–1787

Rebellions and protests against state tax policies, primarily in western Massachusetts, continued. Shays supporters ('Regulators') seized weapons from the Springfield arsenal and marched toward the State House, but a privately hired militia dispersed them at the arsenal, effectively ending the rebellion. The National Park Service states that viewing this rebellion as one of the catalysts leading to the Constitutional Convention and the present U.S. government structure is a broad consensus among historians. The number of participants and casualties is not recorded in the consulted pages.

  1. Author Talk on Shays' Rebellion - Springfield Armory National Historic Site (NPS) · Government Public History — https://www.nps.gov/spar/learn/news/author-talk-shays-rebellion.htm

5. Modern Events in the Literature

American Civil War Civil War · 1861–1865

It is a civil war between the Confederacy (the North) and the United States (the South). The long-cited figure of 620,000 deaths has been criticized for limitations: neither side had standardized medical records, and the Confederate death toll of 258,000 rested on incomplete combat reports and rough estimates of disease deaths. J. David Hacker of Binghamton University estimated approximately 750,000 (range 650,000–850,000) using the 1870 Census by comparing the disappearance of U.S.-born men and women. This estimate includes disease and guerrilla warfare deaths as well as roughly 50,000 civilian deaths; it has also faced criticism over its assumptions about civilian figures and female mortality.

ItemOfficial FiguresIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsTraditional estimate approx. 620,000 (not a complete count — incomplete medical records)Hacker census estimate approx. 750,000 (650,000–850,000)

Data record: Corresponds to the COW 'U.S. Civil War of 1861-1865' entry (see table). The definitions of COW combat deaths and Hacker's excess mortality estimates differ.

  1. New analysis suggests Civil War took bigger toll than previously estimated - Binghamton University Research News · University research report — https://discovere.binghamton.edu/news/civilwar-3826.html

Tulsa Race Massacre (Greenwood) Uprising · 1921

From May 31 to June 1, 1921, for approximately 18 hours, white mobs attacked the predominantly Black Greenwood district of Tulsa, Oklahoma. The Oklahoma Historical Society Encyclopedia describes the destruction of more than 1,000 homes and businesses and the burning of Greenwood; it presents a credible death toll estimate of 50–300. Because the series classification system lacks a 'collective violence' category, this is placed under 'Uprising,' but the actors were not an uprising against the government but attacks by civilian mobs.

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Rebellions of Lower and Upper Canada, 1837–38 Uprising · 1837–1838

In Lower Canada (present-day Quebec), the Patriots led by Louis-Joseph Papineau took control of the elected Legislative Assembly in the 1820s and demanded control over revenue and expenditures and responsible government against the authority of the British governor and the non-elected Council of Château Clique, but London rejected these demands. The combination of an agricultural depression in the 1830s and tensions between English- and French-speaking urban communities led to the first armed clashes in November 1837; after being defeated by British regular troops and English-speaking volunteers, looting and arson followed against French-speaking villages. In November 1838, a second uprising occurred with the support of American volunteers but was soon suppressed. In Upper Canada (present-day Ontario), William Lyon Mackenzie assembled about 1,000 people at Montgomery's Tavern in Toronto in December 1837 to oppose the Family Compact oligarchy, and after exchanging gunfire with loyalist militia on December 5 on Yonge Street, his forces dispersed. On the loyalist side, there were approximately 120 black soldiers under the command of Major Samuel Jarvis. Mackenzie proclaimed a republic on Navy Island in the Niagara River and withdrew on January 14, 1838. Following the rebellions, the Durham Report recommended the unification of the two colonies, leading to the establishment of the Province of Canada in 1841, which in turn led to the introduction of responsible government. Historians are divided over the support base and necessity of the rebellion: some interpret it as 'an inevitable result of an undemocratic colonial regime,' while others see it as 'bloody violence that delayed reform.'

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Casualties of the Two Uprisings in Lower CanadaOfficial tally not confirmed325 (of whom 27 British soldiers, the rest rebels) — The Canadian Encyclopedia (Buckner)
Casualties of Early Clashes in Upper CanadaOfficial tally not confirmed3 (2 rebels, 1 loyalist) — The Canadian Encyclopedia

Data Record: Events since 1816 do not appear in the table because neither COW civil war data, Archigos, nor NAVCO treat the pre-independence colonial period as a Canadian national unit.

  1. Rebellions of 1837–38 — The Canadian Encyclopedia (Historica Canada, P. A. Buckner) · Public History Encyclopedia — https://www.thecanadianencyclopedia.ca/en/article/rebellions-of-1837

North-West Resistance Uprising · 1885

Amid overlapping anxieties among Plains Indigenous peoples (Cree, Siksika, etc.) facing starvation due to the bison's extinction, Métis awaiting land rights guarantees for riverbank settlements, and white settlers resenting changes to the railway route and poor harvests, the Métis called Louis Riel back from his U.S. exile in 1884. On March 8, 1885, the Métis adopted a ten-point 'Bill of Rights,' and on March 18–19 they established a provisional government at Batoche, appointing Riel as representative and Gabriel Dumont as military commander. At the Battle of Duck Lake on March 26, North-West Mounted Police and volunteers retreated, and on April 2 at Frog Lake, Cree warriors killed nine people including Indian Agent Rae. The federal government dispatched approximately 5,000 troops west by rail; after engagements at Fish Creek (April 24) and Cut Knife (May 2), the resistance collapsed in the Battle of Batoche from May 9 to 12. Riel surrendered on May 15, was tried for treason, and was hanged in Regina on November 16; on November 27, six Cree and two Assiniboine were hanged at Battleford. Riel's execution provoked widespread protest in Quebec. The term itself is contested — Indigenous studies scholars and many historians use 'resistance' to denote a reaction against colonization and 'rebellion' to denote organized armed opposition to an existing government; this book uses both designations.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Casualties at the Battle of Duck Lake9 volunteers · 3 police5 Métis · 1 Indigenous — The Canadian Encyclopedia
Resistance Casualties at the Battle of Batoche51 (reported by Middleton)Reported figures are frequently disputed — The Canadian Encyclopedia (no definitive independent figure)
Government Troop MobilizationApproximately 5,000 (including about 1,700 from the West)No separate count verified beyond the same description

Data record: The event is not recorded in COW National War v5.1 or UCDP (1946–) as a Canadian action; the data table shows only two overseas civil wars linked to Canada, both of which ended in 1982.

  1. North-West Resistance — The Canadian Encyclopedia (Historica Canada, B. Beal·R. Macleod) · Public History Encyclopedia — https://www.thecanadianencyclopedia.ca/en/article/north-west-rebellion

The Winnipeg General Strike and 'Bloody Saturday' Strike · 1919

After the First World War, amid unemployment and rising prices, Winnipeg construction and metal workers demanded collective bargaining rights and wage increases; when negotiations broke down, the Winnipeg Trades and Labour Council declared a general strike on May 15, 1919. Within hours, about 30,000 workers stopped work, with police, firefighters, postal workers, and telephone workers joining in. A 'Committee of One Thousand' organized by businesspeople and politicians characterized the strike as a revolutionary plot; The Canadian Encyclopedia notes that there was little evidence to support claims of Bolshevik or Eastern European immigrant leadership. The federal government amended immigration legislation to allow the deportation of British-born immigrants and broadened the definition of seditious intent in the Criminal Code, and on June 17 arrested the strike leaders. On June 21, Royal North-West Mounted Police charged and fired into a silent march, wounding about 30 people and killing two ('Bloody Saturday'). The strike was declared over on June 25, and seven leaders were sentenced to six months to two years for plotting to overthrow the government. Some participants later took part in founding the Cooperative Commonwealth Federation (CCF, predecessor of the modern New Democratic Party).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
StrikersNot confirmedApprox. 30,000 — The Canadian Encyclopedia
Casualties of Bloody SaturdayNot confirmed2 killed, approx. 30 wounded — The Canadian Encyclopedia

Data record: Because this event predates MM protest data (1990–) and ACLED (2021–), it is not included in the data table.

  1. Winnipeg General Strike of 1919 — The Canadian Encyclopedia (Historica Canada, J. N. Reilly) · Public History Encyclopedia — https://www.thecanadianencyclopedia.ca/en/article/winnipeg-general-strike

October Crisis (FLQ Kidnappings and War Measures Act) Uprising · 1970

The Quebec separatist militant organization Front de libération du Québec (FLQ) carried out more than 200 bombings between 1963 and 1970, killing six people in over 70 armed robberies. On October 5, 1970, the 'Liberation' cell kidnapped British trade commissioner James Cross; on October 10, the 'Chénier' cell kidnapped Quebec Minister of Labour and Immigration Pierre Laporte. At the request of Quebec Premier Bourassa and Montreal Mayor Drapeau, the federal government deployed troops on October 15 and invoked the War Measures Act on October 16 — the only peacetime invocation in Canadian history. Laporte was found murdered on October 17; Cross was released after 59 days, on December 3, in exchange for the kidnappers' safe passage to Cuba. Polls at the time showed majority support for the invocation, but New Democratic Party leader Tommy Douglas and Quebec nationalists and civil libertarians criticized it as an overreaction. Subsequently, the McDonald Commission and Kielb inquiry (1981) ruled RCMP Security Service warrantless break-ins and wiretaps illegal; this led to the creation of CSIS in 1984 and the repeal of the War Measures Act and enactment of the Emergencies Act in 1988.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Arrests under the War Measures Act and Public Order Act497 arrested, 62 charged (435 released)The Quebec government announced compensation of up to $30,000 for approximately 100 wrongfully detained individuals in 1971 — The Canadian Encyclopedia
FLQ Fatalities from 1963–70 AttacksNot confirmed6 killed (over 200 bombings) — The Canadian Encyclopedia

Data record: Not meeting the UCDP/PRIO armed conflict or COW civil war thresholds (25+ combatant deaths per 1,000 population per year), it is not included in the data table. It also does not appear in Archigos or coup data (no change of government).

  1. October Crisis — The Canadian Encyclopedia (Historica Canada, A. McIntosh·C. Cooper) · Public History Encyclopedia — https://www.thecanadianencyclopedia.ca/en/article/october-crisis

Kanesatake Resistance (Oka Crisis) Protest · 1990

When the City of Oka announced in 1989 that it would expand a golf course into the 'Pines' (where the Mohawk cemetery lies) and build townhouses, the Kahnawake Mohawks, whose land claim had been rejected in 1986, were excluded from negotiations. The Mohawks have claimed rights to this land since 1761. When residents blocked roads, the Quebec provincial police (SQ) entered on July 11, 1990; a brief shootout ensued, and Corporal Marcel Lemay was killed (who fired remains unknown). The Kahnawake Mohawks rallied in solidarity, blocking the Mercier Bridge, and on August 20 Canadian troops were deployed under Operation Salon with 4,000 troops. On September 18, 22 soldiers and 75 Mohawks were injured in clashes on Île de la Ristourne, and the standoff ended after 78 days on September 26. The federal government purchased the Pines, canceling development; however, no protected area designation or land transfer took place. Many local residents were angry at the blockade (10,000-strong march in Châteauguay, rocks thrown at evacuation vehicles), and the national Indigenous community staged solidarity blockades. The crisis led to the creation of the Royal Commission on Aboriginal Peoples in 1991.

'Freedom Convoy' Occupation and Invocation of the Emergency Act Protest · 2022

In January–February 2022, truckers protesting pandemic restrictions occupied downtown Ottawa for nearly a month and blocked border crossings including the Ambassador Bridge. Prime Minister Justin Trudeau invoked the Emergencies Act on February 14, 2022 — the first time since its enactment in 1988. The Public Order Emergency Commission (Chair Justice Paul Rouleau) appointed under the Act concluded in its final report on February 17, 2023 that 'a very high threshold' had been met while noting that 'the evidence is not overwhelming and reasonable people could reach a different conclusion.' The commission identified a cascade of police failures and Ontario's refusal to participate in tripartite talks, issuing 56 recommendations. Some legal scholars also criticized the commission for exceeding its fact-finding mandate by assessing whether the invocation criteria had been met (Goldenberg, Manitoba Law Journal).

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

The 1763 Berbice Uprising (Coffy's Rebellion) 1763–1764

Berbice was a small colony owned by the private company 'Berbice Association', with a total population of less than 4,000 including slaves, and plantations primarily growing cotton, coffee, and cocoa rather than sugar cane. Amid a severe reduction in white garrison forces due to epidemics and inadequate food rations, the first uprising broke out on February 23, 1763, at the MacJacket's farm of Madalena, followed by the full-scale uprising beginning on February 27 at the Hollandia plantation along the middle Berbice River, where Coffy (Kofi) was elected leader and Accara as deputy commander. The reasons cited by the rebels were the brutal actions of a specific plantation owner, with food shortages also mentioned. After the Battle of Fairburn in March, Governor von Hohenheim abandoned Fort Nassau and retreated to the mouth of the river but held out at Danderahet with reinforcements from Suriname (94 men) and Sint Eustatius (154 men). Coffy proposed dividing the colony into north and south to the governor. Following the defeat at the Second Battle of Danderahet on May 13, divisions deepened between African-born and Creole groups as well as African 'ethnic' groups, and after a confrontation over command, Coffy took his own life. His successor Atta waged guerrilla warfare from the Wiki Creek but the uprising dispersed when Dutch troops numbering 660 arrived on January 1, 1764, amid famine and dysentery. Subsequent brief interrogations served as trials, many were executed, and a pardon arrived on December 15, 1764, at the request of the governor. The main sources for the narrative are Dutch records such as the governor's letters and rebel correspondence and interrogation records. The National Archives of the Netherlands exhibited a letter from Coffy to the governor dated May 1763 ("We are free"), and NOS reported that dozens of whites and 1,800 slaves died in this uprising. The start date of the uprising is Guyana's national day.

  1. 1763: Outline of events — Stabroek News (2013-02-27) · Major media (Guyanese daily, history series) — https://stabroeknews.com/2013/02/27/news/guyana/1763-outline-of-events
  2. 'Wij ben vrij': de unieke brieven van een slavenopstand — NOS (2021-01-21) · Public broadcaster (Netherlands) · Affiliation: Media of the former colonial metropole — https://nos.nl/artikel/2365309-wij-ben-vrij-de-unieke-brieven-van-een-slavenopstand

5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

The Demerara Slave Rebellion of 1823 Uprising · 1823

On July 7, 1823, Governor John Murray of the Demerara-Essequibo colony received instructions from the British government to improve the treatment of enslaved people but did not promulgate them for several weeks. As the contents of the instructions were exaggerated in circulation, a belief spread that 'Parliament had voted for emancipation but colonial authorities were hiding it,' and on August 18 more than 10,000 enslaved people on coastal plantations went on strike. Church leaders such as Jack Gladstone and his father Quamina led the uprising, which was largely nonviolent and centered on labor refusal and the arrest of overseers. The rebellion was suppressed within two days, and martial law remained in effect from August 19, 1823 to January 19, 1824. The Georgetown Court Martial tried approximately 200 prisoners, and the bodies of those executed were publicly displayed as examples. John Smith, a missionary of the London Missionary Society, was sentenced to death on charges of incitement but died in prison before his sentence was commuted; opposition to the executions in British public opinion led to a suspension of further executions. The event is cited as an incident that influenced the debate over the abolition of slavery in Britain.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Participating SlavesNot confirmedMore than 10,000 — GlobalSecurity.org
Fatalities during SuppressionNot confirmedEstimated at 100–250 — GlobalSecurity.org
Sentences of Death and ExecutionsTotal of 28 executions recorded by date of court-martial: 2 on 8.26, 2 on 8.27, 4 on 8.28, 6 on 9.6, 9 on 9.12, 4 on 1824.1.9, and 1 on 1.1045 death sentences, 27 executions — GlobalSecurity.org summary paragraph (a difference of 1 within the same page)

Data Record: This is an event in a British colony prior to independence (1966) and thus not recorded in country-level data such as COW, UCDP, or Archigos.

Guatemala

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Kaqchikel Resistance and Departure from Iximche Spanish Conquest · 1524–1530

When Pedro de Alvarado arrived at the capital of the Cakchiquel Kingdom, Iximché, in April 1524, the two kings, Belehe K'at and K'aj Imix, received him. According to an article published in the Penn Museum's house journal, Alvarado demanded that the two kings give him '1,000 sheets of gold leaf worth 15 pesos.' Subsequently, the Cakchiquels turned back against the Spanish, and the same article notes that Spanish persecution of the rebellious Cakchiquels was frequent until 1530. In 1526, a group of mutinous Spanish soldiers burned Iximché, and in August of the same year Bernal Díaz del Castillo passed through the city after fighting Cakchiquel forces hiding in the ravines. The war soon left Iximché abandoned, and Spain founded a new capital, Santiago, in the Almolonga Valley in 1527. The Cakchiquels submitted to Spanish tribute in 1530 but discontent continued; Alvarado suspected that K'aj Imix was planning a new uprising in 1540 and had him hanged. The main sources of the narrative are Spanish records and the Cakchiquel chronicle (the Sololá record), and the specific dates such as the start of the uprising differ across sources. A historical article in the Guatemalan daily Prensa Libre states that the uprising began on August 28, 1524, with K'aj Imix as its leader; it cites the demand for precious metals and the abduction of young women as causes; it notes that K'aj Imix and Kiyavit Ka'ok rose again in 1533 and held out until their imprisonment in 1535, and gives the date of execution as May 28, 1540 (the article cites only a 'chronicle' as its source).

  1. The Ancient Cakchiquel Capital of Iximché — Expedition Magazine, Penn Museum · University museum academic journal · Tone: Archaeology-focused narrative, reliant on Spanish chronicles — https://www.penn.museum/sites/expedition/?p=1654
  2. Un pueblo aguerrido — Prensa Libre (Revista D) · Major media (Guatemalan daily, history article) · Tone: Sources cited only as 'chronicles' — https://www.prensalibre.com/revista-d/un-pueblo-aguerrido/

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Overthrow of the Arbenz Government in 1954 Regime Overthrow · 1954

Jacobo Árbenz Guzmán took office as president on March 15, 1951, and the character list compiled by the U.S. Department of State's Office of the Historian for Foreign Relations of the United States, Guatemala, 1952–1954 records his tenure until June 27, 1954. The preface to the same series characterizes the event as an important case of U.S. use of covert action and notes that an earlier volume published in 1983 did not record 'the role of the CIA in the ouster of a democratically elected president of Guatemala.' Accounts by the Guatemalan government and its supporters, the U.S. government, and subsequent ruling forces differ; this entry follows the official narrative in the U.S. government's documentary series. The Powell & Thyne coup data records the June 29, 1954 event as a 'success.'

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
End of Árbenz Tenure1954-06-27 — U.S. Department of State FRUS 1952–1954 Guatemala character list1954-06-29 coup 'successful' — Powell & Thyne (see data table)

Data Record: In the UCDP conflict 233's list of opposing forces, 'the Carlos Castillo Armas military' is included, so the 1954 event is bundled with the 1949–1995 government-targeted armed conflict under a single conflict number. The preface page of FRUS does not mention the operation name or Castillo Armas.

  1. Foreign Relations of the United States, 1952–1954, Guatemala — Preface, Office of the Historian, U.S. Department of State · Official government documentary series (United States) · Orientation: compiled by the intervening party's government — https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1952-54Guat/preface

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Bloodless Coup of March 1979 (New Jewel Movement Seizes Power) Coup · 1979

According to an article by Captain Michael A. Hanson published in the Marine Corps Association's journal (2022), Maurice Bishop and the 'New JEWEL Movement' overthrew the government of Prime Minister Eric Gairy in 1979, just five years after independence from Britain in 1974. The same article describes subsequent suspensions of constitutional authority, cancellation of elections, regulation of critical media, and agreements on arms and trade with the Soviet Union and others. This account reflects the perspective of a U.S. military authorship, and it is noted that the ruling forces called this a 'revolution'.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
FatalitiesOriginal text of official Grenadian government tally not confirmedNo mention of fatalities in the MCA article

Data Record: Powell & Thyne coup data records this as a successful coup on 1979-03-13. The Archigos list of irregular leadership changes is blank for Grenada, indicating a discrepancy between datasets.

  1. Capt Michael A. Hanson, 'Grenada, 1983 Operation Urgent Fury' — Marine Corps Association (2022-09-10) · Military specialist association journal article · Tendency: U.S. Marine Corps officer perspective, tending to justify intervention — https://www.mca-marines.org/?p=112017

Fall of Bishop and U.S.-led Military Intervention in October 1983 Regime Overthrow · 1983

According to the same MCA article, on October 12, 1983, Deputy Prime Minister Bernard Coard's faction placed Maurice Bishop under house arrest. About a week later, approximately 3,000 supporters freed Bishop, and he and his entourage marched to Fort Rupert, the military headquarters. Troops under General Hudson Austin, accompanied by armored vehicles, fired on the crowd, killing about 40 people, and Bishop and those who supported him were recaptured and executed. Austin established a 'Revolutionary Military Council' to seize control of governance. The United States launched an invasion operation (Operation Urgent Fury) on October 25, citing the safety of approximately 600 U.S. citizens, including medical students, and within about a week, it secured the entire island and Carriacou. The article notes that elections were held four days after the withdrawal of the last U.S. forces on December 19, 1983. The nature of the intervention is described differently by the parties involved — the United States and Eastern Caribbean nations characterized it as protecting their citizens and restoring order, whereas the military council and Cuba at the time regarded it as a violation of sovereignty (the original text from the latter could not be consulted in this round). Global Voices's 30th-anniversary retrospective (2013) records Bishop's execution date as October 19, 1983, and the location as Fort Rupert in St. George's; it describes how Coard's military forces lined up Bishop and labor leaders and ministers against a wall and executed them, and that Coard has consistently denied issuing an order to execute them despite being convicted of murder later. The same article notes that October 25 is designated as 'Thanksgiving Day' in Grenada and that evaluations of the intervention diverge among residents who remember it as 'liberation' and those who commemorate the revolutionary era's achievements.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Port of Spain shootings fatalitiesOriginal figures from Grenadian government not verifiedApprox. 40 — MCA article
US military deaths and injuries19 dead, 89 wounded — US military tally cited in MCA articleIndependent tally original unverified
Grenadian casualties (including civilians)Original figures from Grenadian government not verified45 dead, 377 wounded — MCA article
Cuban casualties, injuries, and prisonersOriginal figures from Cuban government not verified24 dead, 29 wounded, 600 captured — MCA article

Data record: Powell & Thyne data records the 1983-10-14 successful coup, while UCDP/PRIO armed conflict data records the 1983 inter-state conflict between the Grenadian and US governments (government issue, 1 year). The Port of Spain executions and US intervention are split across different datasets.

  1. Capt Michael A. Hanson, 'Grenada, 1983 Operation Urgent Fury' — Marine Corps Association (2022-09-10) · Military professional association journal article · Tendency: US Marine Corps officer perspective, tending to justify the intervention — https://www.mca-marines.org/?p=112017
  2. 5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The Sandinista Revolution and the Fall of the Somoza Regime, 1978–1979 Overthrow of Government · 1978–1979

The 'Sandinista Revolution' chapter of the Library of Congress Country Studies volume Nicaragua states that after the Sandinista guerrilla factions officially united in March 1979, intense fighting ensued, and the FSLN launched its final offensive in May 1979. According to the same chapter, Anastasio Somoza Debayle resigned on July 17, 1979, and the FSLN entered the capital Managua on July 19. The chapter notes that approximately 50,000 people died and 150,000 fled abroad during the revolution. Accounts by the Somoza government and the National Guard, accounts by the FSLN, and accounts in U.S.-government-prepared materials differ; this entry follows the account in the Library of Congress Country Study.

ItemOfficial FiguresIndependent Investigations & Other
Revolutionary-Era DeathsApprox. 50,000 — Library of Congress Country Study (U.S. government prepared)COW Civil War Data '1978–1979 Sandinista Rebellion' combat deaths: 3,000 (see data table) — difference in scope (combat deaths vs. total deaths)
Outward Migration150,000 — Library of Congress Country StudyIndependent tally unconfirmed

Data record: COW treats the 1978–1979 Sandinista rebellion (3,000 combat deaths) and the 1982–1988 Contra War as separate wars, whereas UCDP groups 1977–1990 as a single government-versus-opposition conflict ('Nicaragua Government vs. FSLN/Contras', conflict number 336). There is a large gap between the approximate 50,000 total deaths during the revolutionary period and COW's 3,000 combat deaths.

  1. Nicaragua: The Sandinista Revolution — Library of Congress Country Studies (countrystudies.us copy) · Government-prepared country study (U.S. Library of Congress) · Orientation: U.S. government institution prepared; possible Cold War-era U.S. intervention perspective — https://countrystudies.us/nicaragua/14.htm

2018 Anti-Social Security Reform Protests and Crackdown Protest · 2018–2019

On April 18, 2018 protests, UN News reported on May 11 of the same year that at least 47 people had died, including two police officers and a journalist, and that the UN Human Rights Office (OHCHR) had called for an independent investigation. The National Assembly established a Truth, Justice, and Peace Commission (CVJP) to investigate the deaths, and Panamanian broadcaster TVN reported on May 29, 2018, that the commission had tallied 85 deaths and 997 injuries by May 28, with those figures described as 'subject to investigation and verification.' The same report noted that the commission was composed solely of lawmakers close to President Daniel Ortega and Vice President Rosario Murillo, raising questions about its independence. Human Rights Watch reported on September 5, 2018, citing CIDH figures that approximately 322 people had died and 2,000 had been injured since April, with most dying in crackdowns by police and armed groups; the Nicaraguan government rebutted the OHCHR report as 'subjective and biased,' and on August 31 the Ministry of Foreign Affairs ordered the UN human rights team to leave the country. Nicaraguan daily La Prensa reported on November 15, 2021, that the CIDH had raised the death toll from 328 to 355 (340 men, 15 women, 27 children and adolescents, including 34 police officers) and that the state provided only 'partial information.' The government side blames protest leaders for the violence, while protest groups and human rights organizations blame the state and pro-government armed groups; this entry adjudicates neither.

ItemOfficial FiguresIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths85 (through 2018-05-28, National Assembly-established Truth, Justice, and Peace Commission, 'subject to investigation and verification') — TVN 2018-05-29 reportApprox. 322 (through early September 2018, CIDH) — Human Rights Watch 2018-09-05; 355 (since 2018-04, updated by CIDH 2021-11) — La Prensa 2021-11-15
Injuries997 (through 2018-05-28, CVJP) — TVNApprox. 2,000 (CIDH) — Human Rights Watch
Police DeathsNo single official government figure independently verified34 (CIDH count includes 355) — La Prensa 2021-11-15

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Enriquillo's Baoruco Rebellion Early Spanish conquest and colonization · 1519–1534

In an article published in the daily Acento (September 14, 2025) by Juan de la Cruz, a professor of history at the Autonomous University of Santo Domingo (UASD), the Taino cacique Enriquillo is said to have entered the Bahoruco mountains in 1519 to begin a rebellion that ended in 1534. The same article cites as immediate triggers the harassment of Enriquillo's wife Mencia by the encomendero Andrés de Valenzuela and the rejection of appeals filed with the alcalde mayor Pedro de Badío and Audiencia (Royal Court); as remote causes, it cites the massacre of Xaragua around 1503, the encomienda system, and the sharp decline in the indigenous population. The rebel forces fought a guerrilla war from the mountains, where they knew the terrain well; the Spanish side sent expeditions under Diego de Penalosa (1520–1521), Juan Ortiz de Matienzo (oidor, 1523), Pedro de Badío and Inigo Ortiz (1525), and Hernando de San Miguel (about 80 men, 1526). According to the article, Captain Francisco de Bariño Nuevo arrived in Santo Domingo on February 20, 1533 with 180 men and reached an agreement with Enriquillo on Isla Cabritos in Lago Enriquillo (now Lake Enriquillo). The terms of the agreement included a royal pardon, land, tools, and livestock, and local autonomy under royal authority; in return, the Enriquillo side agreed to cooperate with 'pacification' by returning runaway indigenous people and Africans to their original owners. Enriquillo wrote a letter to Charles V (Charles I) on June 6, 1534 and died on September 27, 1535. The article cites as sources documents from the General Archive of the Indies (AGI), Las Casas's History of the Indies, Oviedo's General and Natural History of the Indies, and Clío 114 (1959) of the Dominican Society of History. Details such as the year (1520 vs. 1521, 180 vs. 200 men) conflict even within the same article.

  1. El cacique Enriquillo y el triunfo de la rebelión del Bahoruco contra los conquistadores españoles entre 1519 y 1534 — Juan De la Cruz, Acento (2025-09-14) · Major media (Dominican daily, contribution by university historian) · Orientation: the author critically evaluates the return of fugitives clause in the agreement; relies on Spanish chronicles and archives — https://acento.com.do/cultura/el-cacique-enriquillo-y-el-triunfo-de-la-rebelion-del-bahoruco-contra-los-conquistadores-espanoles-entre-1519-y-1534-9550114.html

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

April 1965 Constitutionalists Uprising, Civil War, and U.S. Intervention Civil War · 1965

A 2025-04-28 article from the Dominican daily El Día (Angelo Mercedes) describes the uprising of 'Constitutionalist' officers led by Francisco Alberto Caamaño Deñó on April 24, 1965, demanding the return of former President Juan Bosch as the start of the civil war. The same article, citing the U.S. Department of State's Foreign Relations of the United States (FRUS) 1964–1968, Vol. 32, states that President Lyndon B. Johnson ordered the landing of 40,000 troops in Santo Domingo under 'Operation Power Pack' on April 28, and that an OAS-led mediation led to the signing of the 'Acta de Reconciliación' on August 31, establishing a provisional government under Héctor García Godoy. Accounts of the Constitutionalists and so-called 'Loyalists (military/transitional junta)' differ, as do accounts of the U.S. government's justification for intervention (protecting U.S. citizens); this entry makes no judgment on either side. The Powell & Thyne coup data records the April 25, 1965 event (see data table).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations / Other
Combat DeathsDominican government's official single tally unconfirmedCOW Civil War data '1965 Dominican Republic War' combat deaths: 1,469 (see data table)
U.S. Troops Landed40,000 (based on FRUS 1964–1968 Vol. 32) — El Día 2025-04-28 reportIndependent tally unconfirmed

Data Record: The coup data records events of 1962-01-16, 1963-09-25 (overthrow of the Bosch government), 1965-04-25, and 1965-11-22. The September 1963 overthrow of Bosch could not be confirmed from narrative sources in this round and is retained only in the data table.

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Maroon War 1785–1814

Lennox Honychurch's In the Forests of Freedom: The Fighting Maroons of Dominica (2014, self-published; subsequently reissued by Papillote Press and the University of Mississippi Press) covers the 'Maroon War' between maroons—communities of runaway slaves who had settled in the island's interior forests—and British colonial authorities from 1785 to 1814. The publisher's blurb cites Balla, Jacko, Elephant, Angelique, and Calypso as leaders whose resistance seriously threatened the British plantation system. According to an Ian Thomson review published in The Spectator (September 16, 2017), the maroons, who were heavily outnumbered in the First Maroon War of 1785–1786, collapsed when British forces used Kalinago double agents to penetrate their intelligence network; approximately 150 maroons died in this brief conflict, and their leader Balla was captured and hanged in the capital Roseau. The same review notes that the maroons were able to enjoy freedom only after the abolition of slavery in 1838. The sources for the narrative are mainly records from the colonial authorities; records left by the maroons are rare.

  1. Redcoats and runaways — Ian Thomson, The Spectator (2017-09-16), review of Lennox Honychurch's In the Forests of Freedom · Major media (UK weekly review) · Tone: (conservative-leaning) weekly, reviewer's summary — https://spectator.com/article/redcoats-and-runaways/
  2. In the Forests of Freedom: The Fighting Maroons of Dominica — UBC Press book description (University Press of Mississippi, 2019) · University press book information · Tone: Publisher's blurb — https://www.ubcpress.ca/in-the-forests-of-freedom

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The 1981 plot to overthrow the government ('Operation Red Dog') and the coup attempt Coup · 1981

According to a historical article in the American Bar Association Journal, on the night of April 27, 1981, near Fort Pike outside New Orleans, Louisiana, ten men who called themselves mercenaries were arrested as they attempted to board a vessel bound for the Dominican Republic named 'Manana.' They were charged with illegal possession of firearms and explosives and conspiracy to violate the Neutrality Act of 1794; the plan was to bring back former Prime Minister Patrick John, who had stepped down amid a scandal in 1979, instead of Prime Minister Eugenia Charles, and John and the 'Black Revolution Council' were to join the effort, according to the report. Seven pleaded guilty and received sentences of three years each; on June 20, 1981, a federal jury in New Orleans found Stephen Don Black and George Daniel Hawkins guilty and Mike Norris not guilty. The Powell & Thyne coup data record separately an attempted coup in the Dominican Republic on December 19 of the same year (see data table) — the circumstances of this December event could not be verified from sources opened in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations / Other
Arrests in the United States10 persons (U.S. federal investigation) — ABA JournalIndependent tallies unconfirmed

Data record: The April plot was thwarted on U.S. territory and does not appear in coup data; the data contain only the 1981-12-19 attempt. Whether the two events are connected could not be verified in this round. There are no COW or UCDP records of armed conflict.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

1675 Slave Uprising Plot and Executions British Colonial Period (Sugar Plantations) · 1675

A pamphlet published anonymously in London in 1676, Great Newes from the Barbadoes, reports that 'Coromantyn' (Gold Coast, present-day Ghana) slaves had been secretly preparing for an uprising over about three years. According to the pamphlet, they had crowned a venerable Gold Coast native named Coffee king and enthroned him on June 12; after sounding the horns of ivory and bull in unison from several hills to signal the uprising, they planned to set fire to the sugar cane fields and kill the plantation owners. A female house slave of the Gyles Hall household, Anna (recorded elsewhere as Fortuna), overheard the plan and informed her owner; Governor Jonathan Atkins convened a special court (oyer and terminer) presided over by about ten captains and field officers. The pamphlet records that 17 were initially executed for their convictions (6 burned at the stake, 11 beheaded), followed by 25 more executions; five who were denounced took their own lives rather than face trial, and some 60 remained imprisoned awaiting trial. The Maryland University research site that edited and transcribed this document (Slavery Law & Power) summarizes the number of those executed as 'at least 35' and notes that the pamphlet portrayed Anna as a submissive figure and constructed a narrative framework attributing the cause of resistance to specific origin groups. The basis of the account is colonial rulers' records, and the statements of the slaves who planned the uprising are not preserved.

  1. Great Newes from the Barbadoes (London, 1676) Transcription and Commentary — Slavery Law & Power in Early America and the British Empire (Holly Brewer et al., eds.) · Primary source transcription + commentary for university research project · Orientation: Source text is an anonymous pamphlet from colonial rulers (justifying punishment), commentary by academic editors — https://slaverylawpower.org/chapters/restoration-settlements/barbados-slave-rebellion/

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Bussa's Rebellion Uprising · 1816

According to the summary of the UK slavery abolition education project 'Making Freedom', an uprising began on April 14, 1816, at Bailey Plantation in St. Philip Parish. It is known that Bussa, an African-born man, and Nanny Grigg, a house slave, led the uprising. Local forces suppressed the uprising within three days, and the state of siege declared on April 15 was lifted on July 12. The same source states that approximately 1,000 rebels died, 214 were executed afterward, 123 were deported from the island, and one-quarter of the island's sugarcane crops were destroyed. The scale of deaths and executions is presented differently across sources (see below under unconfirmed items).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Rebel DeathsOriginal text of colonial authorities' official tally not confirmedApprox. 1,000 — Making Freedom
Post-Mortem ExecutionsOriginal text of colonial authorities' official tally not confirmed214 — Making Freedom
Deportation from the IslandOriginal text of colonial authorities' official tally not confirmed123 — Making Freedom

Data Record: The 1816 event, being an internal uprising within the colony, is not recorded in COW, UCDP, coups, Archigos, or NAVCO (pre-sovereign state).

  1. Barbados Rebellion – 1816 — Making Freedom (UK slavery abolition education and community project) · Public history education project · Orientation: Educational summary, no citations of primary sources — https://makingfreedom.co.uk/?p=69

1937 Popular Uprising (Protest against Clement Payne's deportation) Uprising · 1937

According to a Jamaica Observer article on 'Today in Barbados History' (2020-07-26), unrest continued for four days starting from July 26, 1937, Monday, when the colonial government deported Clement Payne, a Trinidadian-born labor organizer advocating union formation and labor reforms, back to Trinidad. In Bridgetown, shop window glass was broken and cars and street lamps overturned; the unrest spread to rural areas where village shops and food stores were raided and telephone lines cut. The Royal Barbados Police Force, the Barbados Volunteer Force, and marines and sailors from HMS Apollo intervened to suppress the unrest, resulting in 14 deaths and 47 serious injuries. The article describes this uprising as having triggered indigenous party politics and served as a catalyst for autonomy and independence; Payne was later designated a national hero. July 26 is currently commemorated as a 'day of national significance.'

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsOriginal text of colonial authorities' investigative commission report not confirmed14 — Jamaica Observer (2020)
Upper-middleOriginal text of colonial authorities' investigative commission report not confirmed47 — Jamaica Observer (2020)

Data Record: As a colonial-era event, there are no records in COW, UCDP, NAVCO, or MM. Since independence (1966), Barbados has no record of coups, civil war, or irregular transfers of power, and ACLED records only 0 to 7 protests per year since 2018 (see data table).

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Caracazo — Riots and Crackdowns of February–March 1989 Protest · 1989

According to an Al Jazeera (2003-11-12) report, the 1989 riots in Caracas were triggered by an austerity economic reform package supported by the International Monetary Fund (IMF), and the protests turned into looting as residents from hillside slums descended. President Carlos Andrés Pérez imposed a curfew and deployed tanks to the streets, and the military fired on crowds. The case summary of the human rights organization CEJIL, which compiles Inter-American Court of Human Rights precedents, notes that Decree No. 49 of February 28, 1989 suspended several constitutional guarantees for 23 days. The case was referred to the Inter-American Court of Human Rights (petition filed 1995-03-28; referred to the court 1999-06-07), and two rulings were issued on November 11, 1999, and August 29, 2002; the Venezuelan government acknowledged international responsibility, which the court accepted. The victims of the court case include 35 extrajudicial executions, 2 forced disappearances, and 3 injuries. In November 2003, the Chávez government approved compensation of approximately $6 million for the families of the victims. The same report also notes that the timing of the compensation coincided with a pay raise and wage increase for public servants ahead of the possibility of a recall referendum, and some victim families criticized the government for politically exploiting the sacrifices.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsInitial official figure approx. 300; 2003 government announcement approx. 1,000 — Al Jazeera (2003)Human rights organizations estimate 'far more' (specific figures not stated) — Al Jazeera (2003)
Victims of the Inter-American Court of Human Rights CaseState Acknowledged International Responsibility35 extrajudicial executions, 2 forced disappearances, 3 injuries — CEJIL case summary

Data Record: For the corresponding year's records in protest data such as Mass Mobilization (MM), see the data table. The 1989 event does not appear in coups, Archigos, or COW.

  1. Relief for Venezuelan riot-hit families — Al Jazeera (2003-11-12) · Major media (international broadcaster) · Orientation: Qatari state-funded broadcaster — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2003/11/12/relief-for-venezuelan-riot-hit-families
  2. Caso Caracazo — CEJIL SUMMA (Inter-American Human Rights System case database) · Human rights organization case database · Orientation: operated by a human rights organization representing victims (CEJIL) — https://summa.cejil.org/en/entity/bbgtrtfj4chdj9k9

Protests and Crackdowns Following the 2024 Presidential Election Protest · 2024–2025

According to the press release for Human Rights Watch's April 30, 2025 report Punished for Seeking Change, after the July 28, 2024 presidential election, electoral authorities announced Nicolás Maduro's reelection, and independent observer missions including the UN expert panel and the Carter Center raised serious concerns about the results. In the ensuing nationwide protests, 24 protesters and bystanders died, and HRW stated that it had collected evidence that security forces were involved in some of the killings while 'colectivos'—armed pro-government groups—were involved in others. Security forces initially responded with tear gas and arrests, and later colectivos attacked and intimidated protesters. More than 2,000 people were detained on charges of protesting, criticizing the government, or supporting the opposition, and hundreds were prosecuted under broad charges such as 'incitement to hatred' and 'terrorism.' The Attorney General's Office stated that it had released hundreds of people, but many remain subjects of criminal investigation. HRW interviewed 101 individuals between July 2024 and April 2025. The government did not accept these findings.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsGovernment figures unconfirmed24 — HRW (2025-04-30)
DetentionsAttorney General's Office: hundreds released (total number of detentions not stated) — cited by HRWMore than 2,000 — HRW (2025-04-30)

Data Record: ACLED annual counts of protests and political violence are referenced in the data table.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Slave Rebellions and Escapes in 1765 and 1768 1765, 1768

A reader's column published in the Belizean daily Amandala (Rolando Cocom, 2018-07-28) cites historian Assad Shoman's A History of Belize, 13 Chapters (2000 revised edition, p. 51), reporting that in 1765 slaves owned by Thomas Cook in Jamaica killed their master and a carpenter, robbed the house, and fled to the forest; one settler wrote that 'it is not known when it will end.' The same column, citing the same book, reports that in 1768 twenty-three armed male slaves left New River for Spanish territory, and business at the settlements was expected to cease as more were expected to follow (no death toll is recorded for the 1768 incident). The evidence consists of settler correspondence; records from the rebels' side do not survive.

  1. Dear Editor — Rolando Cocom, Amandala (2018-07-28), Shoman(2000)·Burdon『Archives of British Honduras』 cited · Major media (Belizean daily) reader's column · Orientation: Includes author's interpretation; secondary citation — https://amandala.com.bz/news/?p=306612

The 1773 Belize Old River Slave Rebellion 1773

An Amandala article (2018-08-25) describes the 1773 Belize Old River rebellion, drawing on letters in John Alder Burdon's Archives of British Honduras and Evan X Hyde's Knocking Our Own Ting (1969). Captain David's letter to Admiral Rodney in June 1773 reports that rebels had killed six slave owners and that about fifty were armed with sixteen muskets and cutlasses by mid-June. The preceding Cocom column, citing Shoman (2000, p. 52), states that the rebels seized five settlements in May. The rebellion was suppressed, and Captain Jude was dispatched to intercept survivors. A letter from the Baymen Committee dated October 11, 1773 counted nineteen survivors, eleven of whom reached a Spanish outpost (Spanish Lookout) on the Rio Hondo. All evidence consists of British military and settler correspondence.

  1. 1779, between 1773 and 1798 — Amandala (2018-08-25), based on Burdon『Archives』·Hyde(1969) · Major media (Belizean daily) historical article · Orientation: Requotes letters of colonial authorities; newspaper editorial line is decolonial nationalism — https://amandala.com.bz/news/?p=307187
  2. Dear Editor — Rolando Cocom, Amandala (2018-07-28) · Major media (Belizean daily) reader's column · Orientation: Includes author's interpretation; secondary citation — https://amandala.com.bz/news/?p=306612

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

1820 Slave Rebellion (Will & Sharper) Uprising · 1820

According to an Amandala guest column (2018-07-28), slaves Will and Sharper led a rebellion with a substantial number of slaves in 1820, and a state of emergency was declared in the settlement. The same column lists 1821 as a year of rebellion but does not provide details. No death toll records appear in the newly accessed materials.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Scale of Participation and DeathsSettlement authorities' tally unconfirmed'Substantial number' — Amandala column (no specific figures)

Data Record: As an event of the colonial period, it is absent from COW, UCDP, coups, and NAVCO.

  1. Dear Editor — Rolando Cocom, Amandala (2018-07-28) · Major media (Belize daily newspaper) guest column · Tone: Includes columnist's interpretation — https://amandala.com.bz/news/?p=306612

1919 Demobilized Soldiers' Uprising Uprising · 1919

According to an Amandala editorial (2017-03-18), black demobilized soldiers from British Honduras who had served in the British Army during World War I rose up on July 22, 1919, angry over the treatment they received abroad, the cold reception upon return, and delayed pay. More than 3,000 residents of Belize City, including women, joined them. The unrest lasted two days, July 22–23; other returning soldiers led by Samuel Hines stopped the looting, and British warships arrived the next day, with U.S. ships arriving a few days later. In subsequent trials, six Belizeans were sentenced to varying terms of imprisonment. A 2026 July Amandala article (provided by XTV News) commemorating the 107th anniversary notes that up to approximately 3,000 participants, a large portion of Belize City's population at the time, took part. The account relies on commemorative and editorial pieces from Belizean newspapers, and records of colonial authorities' investigations could not be confirmed in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Participating CrowdColonial authorities' tally unconfirmedMore than 3,000 — Amandala (2017, 2026)
Imprisonment Sentences6 (trials) — Amandala (2017) accountIndependent tallies unconfirmed

Data Record: As an event of the colonial period, there is no record in the datasets. Since independence (1981), Belize has no records in COW, UCDP, coups, Archigos, or NAVCO, and only ACLED protest counts exist (see data table).

5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

Gas War (Guerra del Gas) — October 2003 El Alto uprising and presidential resignation Overthrow of Government · 2003

According to an Equal Times report (ITUC-affiliated outlet, 2013), President Gonzalo Sánchez de Lozada's plan in October 2003 to export Bolivian natural gas through Chilean ports to the United States and Mexico was the trigger for protests. Perceptions that Chile had severed Bolivia's access to the Pacific fueled opposition. El Alto, attached to La Paz, was a major site of clashes. Sánchez de Lozada resigned on October 17, 2003, and left for the United States. The same report notes that subsequent indictments against the president, cabinet ministers, and five military commanders have stalled due to political asylum or overseas residence by many defendants, and in October 2011 five military commanders and two former ministers were sentenced to 3–15 years' imprisonment.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsGovernment-confirmed figures unverified (official documents could not be accessed for this round)58 as of the resignation on 2003-10-17 (mostly El Alto residents) — Equal Times (2013)
InjuriesUnconfirmedOver 400 — Equal Times (2013)

Data Record: This is a popular uprising and suppression event that does not meet the UCDP/PRIO armed conflict criteria, so it does not appear in the COW or UCDP fields of the modern data table. Because the president's resignation was not a coup, it is also not included in Powell & Thyne's list of coups.

  1. Equal Times, "Bolivia's gas war ten years on" (2013) · Media · Affiliation: Labor movement (ITUC-supported outlet) — https://www.equaltimes.org/bolivia-s-gas-war-ten-years-on

Post-2019 Election Crisis — Sacaba and Senkata Events Protests · 2019

The Independent Expert Group on Bolivia (GIEI-Bolivia), established under an agreement with the Inter-American Commission on Human Rights (CIDH), released its final report in La Paz in August 2021. According to a DW report (reprinting El Mostrador, 2021-08-18), the investigation covered the late Evo Morales administration through the early transitional government of Jeanine Áñez; GIEI member Patricia Tapa told reporters that at least 37 people had died nationwide. On November 15, 2019, near Sacaba outside Cochabamba, 11 people died, and four days later on November 19 (AFP report), 11 died at the Senkata gas facility in El Alto. The GIEI classified both events as massacres and stated that forensic analysis could suggest some deaths were summary executions. President Luis Arce announced compensation for victims and pledged to pursue a trial of the Áñez government. Former transitional president Áñez demanded respect for the constitution and a fair trial under due process; at the time of reporting, she was in preventive detention. Whether this crisis should be viewed as a 'coup' or as 'civil resistance against electoral fraud' divides accounts among the parties involved, and according to reports, GIEI's mandate did not include that judgment.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths (nationwide during investigation period)Government-confirmed figures unverifiedAt least 37 — GIEI-Bolivia final report (2021), DW report
Deaths (Sacaba 2019-11-15 / Senkata 2019-11-19)UnconfirmedEach 11 — GIEI-Bolivia final report (2021), DW and AFP reports

Data Record: Government vs. protesters clashes do not meet the UCDP armed conflict criteria. Whether Morales's resignation in November 2019 is included in Powell & Thyne's list of coups follows the modern data table (not adjudicated in the narrative).

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Resistance of Quilombo dos Palmares and Zumbi Portuguese colonial period (northeastern sugar plantations) · 17th century ~ 1695

In the interior of present-day Alagoas, the settlement of Quilombo dos Palmares, founded by runaway slaves from plantations, existed throughout the 17th century. According to an explanatory resource at California Polytechnic State University, in 1678 the Governor of Pernambuco offered freedom to fugitive slaves if Palmares submitted to Portuguese rule; leader Ganga Zumba accepted the offer, but Zumbi, who had commanded military operations since 1675, rejected it on the grounds that other Africans remained enslaved. Subsequently, an expedition led by Domingos Jorge Velho under orders from the governor destroyed the settlement, and Zumbi was captured and executed on November 20, 1695. The population of the settlement is variously reported as approximately 20,000 to over 30,000, and details of the year of its fall (1694) and the circumstances of Zumbi's capture also differ across sources. Brazil commemorates November 20 as 'Day of Black Consciousness.' The basis for the narrative primarily relies on later scholarship dependent on colonial authorities' records; the records of Palmares residents themselves do not survive.

  1. Zumbi Dos Palmares: Why He Matters & His Legacy — Cal Poly (California Polytechnic State University) Explanatory Resource · College educational/explanatory material · Tone: Resistance-centered narrative for commemorative and educational purposes — https://grandavehousing.calpoly.edu/news/zumbi-dos-palmares-why-he

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The 1964 Military Coup and Political Violence under the Military Regime (1964–1985) Coup · 1964–1985

On March 31–April 1, 1964, the military overthrew the government of President João Goulart, and a military regime continued until 1985. The National Truth Commission (Comissão Nacional da Verdade, CNV), established by law in 2011 and beginning operations in 2012, investigated human rights violations from 1946 to 1988 and released its final report in December 2014. According to Human Rights Watch's summary, the report confirmed 434 deaths and disappearances (191–192 deaths, 210 disappearances, of which 33 were later found), identified 377 individuals responsible for the violations, and stated that nearly 200 of them were still alive. This list exceeds the 362 compiled by the previous Special Commission on Political Deaths and Disappearances (CEMDP). However, CEMDP chair Eugênia Gonzaga viewed the count of 434 as a 'conservative' figure in an interview in 2024, arguing that it omitted rural workers and indigenous peoples due to the criteria of the 1995 law. The military authorities at the time characterized this as a response to subversive forces.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Political Deaths and Disappearances434 — National Truth Commission final report (2014, national institution)362 — previous CEMDP tally / CEMDP chair assessed 434 as an undercount (2024, figure not stated)
Individuals Identified as Responsible for Violations377 — National Truth Commission final report (2014)Nearly 200 survivors — Human Rights Watch (2014)

Data Record: The coup chronology (coups_pt, etc.) and Archigos leader changes are inserted into data\BRA.json by the generator as tables. Deaths from repression under the military regime do not meet the COW civil war criteria (1,000 combat deaths) and therefore do not appear in the civil war list.

  2. Comissão aponta 377 responsáveis por crimes durante a ditadura — Folha Vitória (2014) · Brazilian regional media · Orientation: Report of the report release (neutral delivery) — https://www.folhavitoria.com.br/politica/comissao-aponta-377-responsaveis-por-crimes-durante-a-ditadura/

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

The 1791 Supérieur Slave Uprising 1791

According to the PMA's history and archaeology page, on June 1, 1791, the slaves of Supérieur rose up upon hearing news that slaves in Martinique had been freed, demanding freedom. The same page notes that this attempt was suppressed quickly, and the leaders were captured and beheaded in the town square. A second uprising occurred on June 23 but ended soon after; subsequently, fugitive slaves formed a community called 'Fond Gens Libres' among the rugged terrain at Gros Piton's base, known as 'brigands.' The same page also notes that the slaves resisted through flight, disruption of plantation labor, and raids on plantations.

  1. Saint Lucia Pitons Management Area — History & Archaeology (government-managed agency page) · Government institution public history page · Orientation: national heritage narrative perspective; some years omitted in dates — https://pma.govt.lc/en/pma/history-archaeology

The 1795–1796 Brigand War and the Battle of La Batache 1795–1796

According to the same PMA page, on April 18, 1795, a British force of about 1,000 men left Vieux Fort and attacked Supérieur, which was held by French forces and the brigands (Battle of La Batache). The brigands fought using guerrilla tactics, and the British, whose numbers had dwindled, retreated to Vieux Fort. The British called them 'brigands' (bandits), while the French revolutionary government viewed them as combatants on the republican side; the name itself reflects the perspectives of the parties involved. There is a description that suppression of remaining brigands followed the British re-landing and appointment of John Moore as governor of the military government in 1796, but this could not be verified by opening the relevant original text in this round (see list of unconfirmed items).

  1. Saint Lucia Pitons Management Area — History & Archaeology · Government institution public history page · Orientation: national heritage narrative perspective — https://pma.govt.lc/en/pma/history-archaeology

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The 1934–1935 Caribbean Labor Disturbances and the Saint Lucia Strike Protest · 1935

In his 2024 article, Seifert includes Saint Lucia in the region covered by strikes and riots in the English-speaking West Indies of 1934–1939, noting that events in small territories such as Saint Lucia were not widely reported in Britain during the early strikes of 1934–35. The abstract of the same article states that the Saint Lucia port is used as a case study. Because no specific details of the event's course or casualties appear on the current round's preliminary abstract page, the specifics are not described.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths · InjuriesColonial authorities' official tally; original text unconfirmedNo figures in Seifert (2024) abstract

Data Records: Saint Lucia is absent from COW, UCDP, Powell & Thyne, Archigos, NAVCO, and Mass Mobilization. 1935 lies either before the observation windows of most of these datasets or outside their scope as a colony. ACLED records only protests since 2018 (the peak year was 2022 with 16 events; one death in 2024).

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Narrative survey pending — not yet written.

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

First Carib War, 1772–1773 1772–1773

According to the King's College London Special Collections exhibition on Caribbean indigenous peoples (Caribbean exhibition), Saint Vincent was ceded from France to Britain by the Treaty of Paris in 1763; at the time, the island was inhabited by approximately 100 Carib families and some 2,000 so-called 'Black Caribs' (citing records by Bryan Edwards). Because much of the land suitable for sugarcane cultivation was occupied by Black Caribs, conflict arose with British settlement. Fighting between Caribs and settlers began in September 1772 and ended in a stalemate in February of the following year; both sides then signed a treaty guaranteeing the Caribs' lands in exchange for their recognition of British sovereignty. The same exhibition notes that the treaty included a clause prohibiting 'unfair intercourse' with French-held islands, but states that the Caribs assisted France in retaking the islands in 1779.

  1. King's College London Special Collections, 'St Vincent' (Caribbean exhibition — Indigenous peoples) · University library special collections exhibition (based on the UK Foreign Office historical collection) · Orientation: relies heavily on British colonial records (Edwards 1807) — https://kingscollections.org/exhibitions/specialcollections/caribbean/indigenouspeoples/stvincent

Second Carib War, 1795–1796, and Forced Relocation of the Garifuna 1795–1797

According to the same exhibition, in 1795 France armed and supported a joint uprising by French settlers and Caribs on the island. The Black Carib leader Joseph Chatoyer was killed early in the war, but the uprising continued until June 1796, when the rebel side was defeated. The defeated Black Caribs were collectively relocated to nearby Baliceaux Island; their numbers, initially over 4,000, rapidly declined to around 2,000 due to overcrowding and disease. In 1797 they were relocated again to Roatán off the coast of Honduras, and many soon moved to the mainland to become today's Garifuna communities in Honduras, Belize, Guatemala, and Nicaragua. British accounts portray this as the suppression of a rebellion, while Garifuna communities and the Saint Vincent government remember it as a history of resistance and expulsion (descriptions exist stating that Chatoyer was designated the first national hero of Saint Vincent, but the government's original text could not be accessed in this round).

  1. King's College London Special Collections, 'St Vincent' (Caribbean exhibition — Indigenous peoples) · University library special collection exhibition · Orientation: dependent on British colonial records, Garifuna oral traditions not reflected — https://kingscollections.org/exhibitions/specialcollections/caribbean/indigenouspeoples/stvincent

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

December 1979 Union Island Uprising Uprising · 1979

According to a UPI article dated March 10, 1981, a Rastafarian group led by Lennox Charles attempted to seize Union Island in the Grenadines on December 7, 1979. The attempt failed within a day due to lack of training and weapons and was suppressed with the support of 48 soldiers airlifted from Barbados. The same article reported that a High Court jury found Camillo Adams and Lenford Ramie guilty of robbery and an attempted bombing of the Union Island police station, four others had previously been convicted (three acquitted, one hung jury), and Lennox Charles was among those convicted. The motives of the uprising side (such as discontent over marginalization in southern Grenadines) could not be verified from the original sources in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
FatalitiesOriginal Saint Vincent government tally unverifiedNo mention of casualties in UPI (1981) article
Foreign-Deployed TroopsGovernment announcement original text unverified48 Barbadian soldiers — UPI (1981)

Data Record: Not recorded anywhere in Powell & Thyne coups, Archigos, UCDP, NAVCO, or Mass Mobilization (appears to have been an attempt to seize power that failed to extend beyond part of the island and thus fell short of criteria in each dataset). ACLED records only from 2018 onward, with 2 deaths in 2020.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

1626 Bloody Point Massacre (Attack on Kalinago Community) Early Colonial Period · 1626

According to the commentary on 'Historic St Kitts' run by the National Archives of Saint Kitts and Nevis, Thomas Warner returned to Saint Kitts in August 1626 with about 100 English settlers, enslaved Africans, and provisions. The Kalinago leader Tegreman, wary of the growing English presence, sought assistance from neighboring islands; a Kalinago woman living in the settlement informed Warner of this (the source of this anecdote is not cited in the commentary). Warner's side attacked first while the Kalinago were brewing cassava beer and preparing for war, and Tegreman was killed. Some allied forces retreated to their own islands by sea, and the few Kalinago remaining on the island persisted into the late 17th century but in small numbers. The same commentary does not give a total death toll; the commonly cited figure of 'about 2,000' could not be confirmed from primary sources in this round. English and French settler records describe it as a preemptive response to planned native attacks, while the modern government of Saint Kitts and Nevis and descendants of Caribbean indigenous peoples remember it as a massacre (commemoration of the 400th anniversary in 2026).

  1. National Archives St Kitts Nevis, Historic St Kitts — 'Bloody Point' · Public history commentary by the National Archives · Orientation: reconstruction based on colonial sources (John Hilton), absence of Kalinago records — https://historicstkitts.kn/places/bloody-point
  2. SKNIS (Saint Kitts and Nevis Information Service), report on the 400th anniversary commemoration of the 1626 Kalinago massacre (2026-08) · Official government report · Orientation: perspective of the current government — 'Thousands', described as a joint atrocity by British and French settlers — https://sknis.gov.kn/?p=103259
