5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

January 1935 Berkeley's Strike and Uprising Uprising · 1935

According to the chronology of the National Archives' 'Historic St Kitts', in December 1934 the Wade estate paid a Christmas bonus of 8 pence per ton of sugar cane, whereas other estates paid 3 pence or nothing. On January 28, 1935, sugarcane cutters at Berkeley's estate demanded 1 shilling per ton from overseer E.D.B. Dovey, who refused; they went on strike, and the march grew to 300–400 people as it passed several estates. Armed police arrested five leaders on the same day. On January 29, Dovey fired his shotgun at protesters, and around 5 p.m., upon request of Lieutenant Duke, the defense force and reserve with live ammunition were deployed; the governor requested a warship from Bermuda and police reinforcements from Antigua and read the Riot Act. After repeated warnings in the evening, shots were fired, killing three workers. On January 30, troops from Antigua arrived, and on January 31, the British warship HMS Leander arrived. The current government of Saint Kitts and Nevis and the National Assembly call this the 'Berkeley's Uprising' and commemorate the dead; records of the colonial authorities at the time describe it as the suppression of a disturbance.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths3 — Joseph Samuel, John Allen, James Archibald (National Archives chronology)3 — Richard Hart (labor history research) also describes it on the same scale; original text cross-checking incomplete
Injuries8 in the text, 9 listed (internal inconsistency in the National Archives chronology)9 injured (based on search summaries; original texts not reviewed)

Data Record: The 1935 event is not recorded in any modern dataset, including COW and Archigos (not meeting the threshold due to the colonial period and small-scale labor unrest). UCDP, NAVCO, Mass Mobilization, and ACLED also have virtually no records for this country, and ACLED only counts a few events: 2 protests after 2018, 2 incidents of political violence, and 1 death.

  1. National Archives St Kitts Nevis, Historic St Kitts — 'Buckley's Strike Timeline' · National Archives public history chronology · Orientation: Memorial perspective of the current government (designated as an uprising) — https://historicstkitts.kn/events/buckley-s-strike-timeline

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The 1980 'Lieutenant Coup' and the Establishment of Military Rule Coup · 1980

The Inter-American Court of Human Rights judgment in Moiwana (2005) states in fact that Desiré Bouterse led a coup in 1980 to establish military rule. A Just Security (2023) op-ed describes the December 1982 killings as occurring 'almost three years after' Bouterse's military coup. The military regime presented its seizure of power as a measure to root out corruption, while the ousted civilian government saw it as an interruption of constitutional rule; these accounts coexist, but the original texts from both sides could not be consulted in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
FatalitiesOriginal text of Surinamese government official tally unconfirmedNo mention of fatalities in sources consulted

Data Record: Powell & Thyne coup data records two events, 1980-02-25 and 1980-08-15, as successful coups. Archigos records that Ferrier was ousted by the military on 1980-08-13, so the February military seizure of power and the August ouster of the president are recorded under different dates.

  1. Inter-American Court of Human Rights, Case of the Moiwana Community v. Suriname, Judgment of June 15, 2005 (Series C No. 124), ¶86(12) · Judicial ruling of an international organization · Orientation: centered on fact-finding in a case brought by the affected community and assessment of state responsibility — https://hrlibrary.umn.edu/iachr/C/124-ing.html
  2. Just Security, 'A Leader is Brought to Justice 41 Years Later in Suriname' (2023-12) · Op-ed for a legal and security-focused online journal · Orientation: human rights and accountability perspective — https://www.justsecurity.org/90983/a-leader-is-brought-to-justice-41-years-later-in-suriname/

The December 1982 Killings (December Murders) Overthrow of the Regime · 1982

According to the Just Security op-ed, on the night of December 8, 1982, soldiers took 15 political opponents from their homes and brought them to Fort Zeelandia in Paramaribo; they were tortured and shot dead, and their bodies were left in a morgue. The victims included labor leaders, four lawyers, four journalists, a businessman, and a former member of parliament. In November 2007, the Attorney General indicted Bouterse and 24 others, leading to the establishment of a special military tribunal (Krijgsraad) composed of civilian judges; however, the ruling party passed a law granting amnesty to the defendants, halting the trial for four years. In 2019, the special military tribunal sentenced Bouterse, then serving as president, to 20 years in prison on charges of planning and ordering the killings; the conviction was upheld in an appeal of the in absentia trial approximately two years later, and the High Court (Hof van Justitie) confirmed the conviction on December 20, 2023. Reports exist that Bouterse's camp has argued that the victims were linked to a Dutch-US invasion plan, while defense attorneys have maintained that it was not a planned murder; however, the original texts of these claims could not be consulted directly in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Fatalities15 — Surinamese court ruling (cited in Just Security op-ed)15 — Just Security op-ed

Data Record: Because it is a single nighttime execution, there are no separate entries for this event in UCDP, COW, or coup data. NAVCO records the 1983–84 anti-Bouterse nonviolent movement as a 'failure'.

  1. Just Security, 'A Leader is Brought to Justice 41 Years Later in Suriname' (2023-12) · Op-ed for a legal and security-focused online journal · Orientation: human rights and accountability perspective — https://www.justsecurity.org/90983/a-leader-is-brought-to-justice-41-years-later-in-suriname/

The Interior War of 1986–1992 and the Moiwana Massacre Civil War · 1986~1992

According to the factual findings of the Inter-American Court of Human Rights, armed opposition forces known as the 'Jungle Commando' began operations in eastern Suriname in 1986; most of its members were Maroons and its leader was Ronnie Brunswijk. Military operations in 1986–87 killed at least 200 civilians, mostly residents of Maroon villages, and approximately 15,000 people fled to Paramaribo and about 8,500 to French Guiana during the same period. On November 29, 1986, in an operation against Moiwana village, state agents and their collaborators killed at least 39 residents and destroyed the village, according to the court; military commander Bouterse confirmed in a press statement that he had ordered the operation. The military seized power again in December 1990. The 1989 Amnesty Law was promulgated on August 19, 1992, pardoning certain crimes committed between January 1, 1985 and August 20, 1992, excluding crimes against humanity. In its June 15, 2005 judgment, the court rejected all five preliminary objections raised by Suriname and recognized jurisdiction over ongoing violations after November 12, 1987. The Surinamese government argued that the attack predated the acceptance of the court's jurisdiction.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Moiwana FatalitiesSurinamese government official tally; original text unverifiedAt least 39 — Inter-American Court of Human Rights factual finding / over 40 — Inter-American Commission on Human Rights claim
Civilian Deaths (1986–87)Surinamese government official tally; original text unverifiedAt least 200 — Inter-American Court of Human Rights factual finding
Displaced Persons (1986–87)Surinamese government official tally; original text unverifiedApprox. 15,000 to Paramaribo, approx. 8,500 to French Guiana — Inter-American Court of Human Rights factual finding

Data Record: The UCDP/PRIO armed conflict data records the government vs. SLA (Liberation Army of Suriname = Jungle Commando) conflict as active for only one year in 1987, differing from the 1986–1992 conflict period described in the judgment. The COW Civil War list contains no entry for Suriname. Archigos and Powell & Thyne data on the December 24, 1990 military ouster of President Shankar correspond to the 're-seizure of power by the military in December 1990' described in the judgment.

  1. Inter-American Court of Human Rights, Case of the Moiwana Community v. Suriname, Judgment of June 15, 2005 (Series C No. 124) · Judicial ruling of an international organization · Orientation: factual findings on the case brought by the affected community, centered on determining state responsibility — https://hrlibrary.umn.edu/iachr/C/124-ing.html

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Calchaquí War — The Great Rising (Gran Alzamiento) Modern Era (Colonial Period) · 1630–1643

Diaguita groups in the Calchaquí Valley (present-day Salta, Tucumán, and Catamarca) resisted Spanish royal conquest and encomienda forced labor from the 1560s onward. In 1630, a 'general uprising of the land' broke out; Chalimín led the southern uprising, and research by the National University of Tucumán describes 1630–1643 as the period of most intense conflict. In historiography, Aníbal Montes's study 'The Great Rising of the Diaguitas, 1630–1643' (1961) is cited as the standard reference.

  1. Mundo de Antes (UNT) — Research paper on the Calchaquí uprising · Academic article · Orientation: Argentine archaeology and ethnohistory — https://publicaciones.csnat.unt.edu.ar/index.php/mundodeantes/article/download/246/227/1786
  2. Histórica (PUCP) — Study of the Calchaquí uprising and colonial rule · Academic article · Orientation: Peruvian academic journal, colonial history perspective — https://revistas.pucp.edu.pe/index.php/historica/article/download/8398/8721

Pedro Bohórquez ('Inca Huallpa')'s uprising and the Third Calchaquí War Modern Era (Colonial Period) · 1658–1667

Around 1658, an adventurer from Andalusia named Pedro Boorques, who claimed to be a descendant of the Incas ('Inca Huallpa'), was accepted as a military leader by the inhabitants of the Calchaqui Valley. He surrendered to the Spanish side in 1659 hoping for a pardon but was sent to Lima and executed in January 1667. Subsequently, the Spanish authorities dispersed surviving residents, allocated them to encomiendas, and forcibly relocated them; the Quilmes group was moved near Buenos Aires, and that place name has remained to this day. The primary narrative source is the Jesuit missionary Hernando de Torres Blancas's Relación Histórica de Calchaquí (1696), which must be read alongside Diaguita accounts because it is a record from the missionary party.

  1. Americanía (Universidad Pablo de Olavide) — Torreblanca's Relación Histórica de Calchaquí and Jesuit narratives · Academic article · Orientation: Critical study of primary sources (Jesuit records) — https://upo.es/revistas/index.php/americania/article/view/10806
  2. Mundo de Antes (UNT) — Research paper on the Diaguita uprising · Academic article · Orientation: Argentine archaeology and ethnography — https://publicaciones.csnat.unt.edu.ar/index.php/mundodeantes/article/download/246/227/1786

5. Modern Events through Literature

State repression and forced disappearances during the military regime (Process of National Reorganization) period Regime Overthrow · 1976–1983

Under the military regime established by the March 1976 coup, large-scale abductions, detentions, and forced disappearances by state institutions took place. After the return to civilian rule, President Alfonsín established the National Commission on the Disappearance of Persons (CONADEP, chaired by Ernesto Sabato), which began its work in December 1983 and published its report Never Again in 1984. The report identified more than 340 secret detention sites across the country and over 9,000 disappeared persons, serving as the basis for subsequent military junta trials. Human rights organizations have estimated the number of disappeared at 30,000, and the discrepancy between official figures and human rights estimates has itself been a subject of public debate since.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Number of Forced DisappearancesOver 9,000 (CONADEP 'Nunca Más', 1984)30,000 (human rights organizations' estimates)
Secret Detention FacilitiesOver 340 (CONADEP, 1984)—

Data Record: The coup itself in 1976 is recorded in the coup data set table. Unilateral violence by the state (disappearances) does not fall under the UCDP state-based armed conflict category and may therefore not appear in the data table.

  1. CONADEP, 'Nunca Más' Report (1984) Digital Edition — Held by Sitios de Memoria Uruguay · Government Report (Truth Commission) · Orientation: Committee established by civilian government, based on testimony of victims — https://sitiosdememoria.uy/sites/default/files/2025-08/nunca-mas-informe-de-la-conadep_f1984.pdf
  2. El Cronista — 38th Anniversary of 'Nunca Más' Article · Major Media · Orientation: Argentine business daily, centrist — https://www.cronista.com/informacion-gral/nunca-mas-a-38-anos-del-informe-clave-en-el-juicio-a-las-juntas/

Social Uprising and Crackdown in December 2001Protests · 2001

In the midst of the economic crisis of December 2001, looting, pot-banging protests, and large-scale street demonstrations spread nationwide; deaths occurred during the declaration of a state of emergency on December 19–20 and the police crackdown. President Fernando de la Rúa resigned on December 20. The figure of 39 deaths (including 10 minors) is most widely cited and was used in government official mourning and compensation discussions. A 2017 document by human rights organization CELS records '34 killed by police repression nationwide,' while some media outlets cite 38 deaths and over 4,500 detentions. CELS notes that it led to a 2016 ruling holding political leaders criminally responsible for orders to suppress the protests.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths39 (used in government mourning and compensation announcements, including 10 minors)34 (CELS 2017 document) / 38 (Marcha)
Detainees—Over 4,500 (Marcha)

Data Record: This period precedes ACLED collection and does not meet the UCDP armed conflict criteria (state vs. organized armed group), so it may not appear in the data table. Inclusion in NAVCO and MM follows the data table.

  1. CELS — CELS's work on the right to protest (document) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: Argentine human rights organization, participant in litigation on behalf of victims — https://cels.org.ar/web/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/EltrabajodelCELSsobreelderechoalaprotesta.pdf
  2. La Jornada — 10 years since 39 deaths in crackdown (2011) · Major Media · Orientation: Mexican left-leaning daily newspaper — https://www.jornada.com.mx/2011/12/21/mundo/027n2mun
  3. BAE Negocios — Proposal for compensation to victims of the December 2001 crackdown (2021) · Major Media · Orientation: Argentine business daily — https://www.baenegocios.com/amp/El-presidente-Alberto-Fernandez-encabezara-manana-al-mediodia-un-homenaje-a-victimas-de-la-represion-de-diciembre-de-2001-20211218-0050/

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Macandal's Maroon Rebellion French Colonial Period (Santo Domingo) · 1751–1758

According to the Library of Congress Country Study on Haiti, mountainous and forested areas of Saint-Domingue were occupied by maroon (runaway slave) communities that raided plantations for food and weapons; some bands numbered in the thousands, but lacked central organization or unified leadership. Among the most famous leaders was François Mackandal, said to have drawn followers through African traditions; his rebellion lasted from 1751 to 1757, with an estimated 6,000 dead. The French authorities burned him at the stake in Cap-Français in 1758. Colonial records describe this as a poisoning plot, while modern Haitian national memory remembers it as the first sign of rebellion against French rule and slavery. Accounts of the date of his execution (January or March 1758) and of his escape from the stake differ across sources, and the tradition is not confirmed by historical records.

  1. Library of Congress Federal Research Division, Haiti: A Country Study (R. Haggerty ed., 1989) — 'French Colonial Rule / Slave Rebellion' · Government research agency country study (public history commentary) · Affiliation: U.S. government publication; original sources of casualty estimates not cited — https://countrystudies.us/haiti/9.htm

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Vincent Ogé Uprising, 1790 1790

According to the same country study, when the Paris National Assembly demanded voting rights for free people of color (gens de couleur) with property and tax requirements, a mulatto rebel force led by Vincent Ogé rose up demanding this. The white colonial assembly rejected it, and white militia forces, reinforced by black volunteers, suppressed the 1790 uprising.

  1. Library of Congress, Haiti: A Country Study — 'Slave Rebellion' · Government research agency country study · Affiliation: U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/haiti/9.htm

Northern Slave Uprising and Haitian Revolution (1791–1804) 1791~1804

According to the country study, in early August 1791, rebel leaders including Boukman, Jean-François, and Jeannot gathered at a Bois-Caïman ceremony led by Boukman, and a week later, on August 22, they attacked settlements in northern areas such as Aquin, Léogâne, Plaisance, and Le Normand. The rebels failed to take the well-defended Cap-Français, and the colonists retaliated harshly against non-whites. It is estimated that about 10,000 blacks and 2,000 whites died in this uprising, with over 1,000 plantations looted or destroyed. In the west and south, mulatto forces led by André Rigaud and Alexandre Pétion fought white militia, French Republic commissioners arrived in 1792, and Spanish and British intervention opened a new phase. The country study notes that the claim that Toussaint Louverture planned the uprising has not been confirmed. The date of the ceremony (August 14 or 21, etc.) varies by chronology and early recorders (French colonists, later Haitian historians), and scholars note that parts of the detailed account of the ceremony have become mythologized in national memory. The subsequent development up to the declaration of independence in 1804 extends beyond the pages checked and confirmed in this round (up to 1792) and is left to the research literature below.

  1. Library of Congress, Haiti: A Country Study — 'Slave Rebellion' · Government research agency country study · Affiliation: U.S. government publication; original sources of casualty estimates not cited — https://countrystudies.us/haiti/9.htm

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Human Rights Violations under Military Rule Following the September 1991 Coup (1991–1994) Coup · 1991–1994

The IACHR's 1995 Haiti Human Rights Report estimates that approximately 3,000 people were killed during the period of military rule following the September 29, 1991 coup that ousted President Aristide, and notes that President Aristide presented a figure of 5,000 deaths through mid-1994. The commission recorded 210 extra-judicial executions between January 31 and May 31, 1994, while the OAS/UN International Civilian Mission recorded 340 between February and June 1994; the total number cannot be confirmed. Regarding the April 1994 Raboteau attack, figures diverge between 15–20 executions (report text), at least 12 confirmed by the OAS/UN mission, and at least 28 according to other sources, with testimony pointing to troops from the Thibault Fort; within the report itself, dates for the Raboteau incident are given differently (March 22 and April 18–22). The military described this as a measure to restore order, while Aristide's camp and human rights organizations characterized it as suppression of supporters.

ItemOfficial StatementsIndependent Investigations / Other
Fatalities under Military Rule (1991–1994)Approx. 5,000 — presented by President Aristide (exiled government), as of mid-1994 (citing IACHR report)Approx. 3,000 — estimated by the IACHR (IACHR 1995)
Fatalities in the Raboteau Incident (1994)Military figures could not be confirmed in this round15–20 (IACHR text) / at least 12 (confirmed by OAS/UN mission) / at least 28 (other sources)

Data record: This period is included in Powell & Thyne coup data and UCDP Conflict 381 (government vs. military faction, 1989–2025). The IACHR's casualty figures represent unilateral violence (killing of civilians by the state), and thus differ in unit from the UCDP/PRIU armed conflict annual counts, which use combat fatalities as the criterion.

  1. Inter-American Commission on Human Rights, Report on the Situation of Human Rights in Haiti (1995), Chapter IV · International organization (OAS) human rights report · Orientation: focuses on documenting violations by the military regime; limited inclusion of military rebuttals — https://hrlibrary.umn.edu/iachr/country-reports/haiti1995-ch4.html

Gang Violence and Weakened State Control (2024) Civil War · 2024

According to figures verified by the UN Human Rights Office (OHCHR), at least 5,601 people were killed, 2,212 injured, and 1,494 kidnapped in gang violence in Haiti in 2024. The death toll exceeded that of 2023 by more than 1,000. In early December in Cité Soleil, Port-au-Prince, at least 207 people were killed on the orders of the leader of the gang Waf Jérémie; the UN reported that the leader blamed voodoo rituals of elderly victims for his son's death. In the same year, 315 lynchings of suspects accused of gang involvement and 281 suspected summary executions by police special forces were also recorded. The government characterizes this as a problem of combating criminal organizations, while the gang coalition regards it as a political force; accounts diverge among the parties involved (accounts from the gang side could not be verified from primary sources in this round).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
2024 Gang Violence FatalitiesOfficial Haitian government tally could not be confirmed in this roundAt least 5,601 (OHCHR verified; UN News 2025-01)
2024 Injuries and KidnappingsNot ConfirmedInjuries: 2,212; kidnappings: 1,494 (OHCHR)

Data Record: The gang coalition Viv Ansanm is recorded as the opponent in UCDP Conflict 381, continuing through 2025. OHCHR figures include all deaths from gangs, police, and vigilante lynchings, a human rights monitoring tally that differs from the UCDP standard for combat fatalities.

  1. UN News, 'More than 5,600 killed in Haiti gang violence in 2024' (2025-01-07) · Official report of an international organization (OHCHR verified figures) · Orientation: UN human rights monitoring perspective; gang-side narrative not included — https://news.un.org/en/story/2025/01/1158781

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Maroon Community at Boggy Peak and the General Roundup of 1687 British Colonial Period (17th century) · 17th century–1687

According to a summary by Mike Dash (2013) based on the work of David Barry Gaspar and others, in the 17th century, runaway slaves in Antigua formed maroon communities around the summit of Boggy Peak, an extinct volcano in the island's interior. In 1687, when authorities ordered a general roundup, one of the recaptured maroons who was convicted of 'rebellious conduct' was sentenced to burning at the stake, and another, who had been responsible for communication, was sentenced to amputation of the legs. No records from the maroon side survive, and the account relies on colonial authorities' court records.

  1. Mike Dash, 'Antigua's Disputed Slave Conspiracy of 1736', Smithsonian Magazine (2013-11-15) · Major media historical commentary (magazine of a museum institution), presenting a list of scholarly sources · Orientation: Based on colonial authorities' court records; no primary records from the enslaved side — https://www.smithsonianmag.com/history/antiguas-disputed-slave-conspiracy-of-1736-117569/

The 1701 Samuel Martin Plantation Slave Uprising British Colonial Period (Early 18th century) · 1701

According to the same commentary, in 1701, 15 newly arrived slaves killed their owner, Captain Samuel Martin, who had failed to grant them a Christmas holiday. Contemporary accounts state that the slaves beheaded him, dipped his head in rum, and shouted victory over it. This account is based on reports by colonial authorities.

  1. Mike Dash, Smithsonian Magazine (2013) · Major media historical commentary · Orientation: Based on contemporary colonial records — https://www.smithsonianmag.com/history/antiguas-disputed-slave-conspiracy-of-1736-117569/

The 1729 Crump Plantation Conspiracy Case British Colonial Period (Early 18th century) · 1729

According to the same commentary, in 1729 a conspiracy among slaves owned by Nathaniel Crump, an Antiguan legislator, was uncovered through the betrayal of one of the slaves. The court held that the aim of the plot was not only to kill the Crump family but also to exterminate all whites on the island; it sentenced three of Crump's slaves to burning and another to hanging, disembowelment, and dismemberment. The court added a warning that 'the plot was far more deeply laid than had been supposed.' No statements from the enslaved side regarding the reality or scale of the conspiracy survive.

  1. Mike Dash, Smithsonian Magazine (2013) · Major media historical commentary · Orientation: based on court records — https://www.smithsonianmag.com/history/antiguas-disputed-slave-conspiracy-of-1736-117569/

The 1736 Conspiracy of Prince Klas (Cort, Cott, Quaku Takyi) British colonial era (early 18th century) · 1735–1736

According to the same commentary, around 1736, 24,400 people, or 85% of Antigua's population, were enslaved, while there were approximately 3,800 whites. Following an economic downturn, drought, and the 1735 earthquake, plantation owners reduced food and housing costs for slaves, while a slave rebellion was underway on neighboring Danish St. John. 'Cort' (Kwaku Takyi, an African name; remembered in modern Antigua as national hero Prince Klas), brought from West Africa at age 10 in 1704, was accused of plotting to blow up the ballroom for a masquerade scheduled for October 1736 with 10-gallon barrels of gunpowder and using this as a signal for slaves from surrounding plantations to advance on the capital from four directions. Suspicion emerged during the nearly three-week postponement of the masquerade, and Justice of the Peace Robert Aboucant investigated; after torture and other interrogation, 32 confessed, and 132 were convicted. Among them, five were hanged in the wheel, six were hanged by chains to starve to death, and 77 were burned at the stake, for a total of 88 executions. The Antiguan government at the time regarded the conspiracy as sufficiently proven, and many historians such as Gaspar follow this view; however, the barrels of gunpowder and armory were never found, and Jason Sharples and Kwasi Konadu raise questions about the credibility of the confessions and the possibility that the 'coronation masquerade' was not a declaration of war but an Akan (Ashanti)-style ceremony to inaugurate a leader. Whether the conspiracy existed and, if it did, whether it targeted the extermination of whites has not been adjudicated by scholars.

  1. Mike Dash, Smithsonian Magazine (2013) · Major media historical commentary (synthesis of academic debates) · Orientation: both sides' theories presented — colonial court records and revisionist scholarship both introduced — https://www.smithsonianmag.com/history/antiguas-disputed-slave-conspiracy-of-1736-117569/

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Guayaquil General Strike and Suppression on November 15, 1922 Protest · 1922

Amid layoffs, wage cuts, and rising prices resulting from reduced cocoa exports (the dollar exchange rate rose from 2 to 5 sucre), Guayaquil workers declared a general strike in early November, with the slogan 'Dollar at 2 Sucre.' On November 15, the government of José Luis Tamayo decided to disperse the march by force. Ecuadorian labor movement history includes accounts referring to this event as the 'baptism of blood' for the labor movement. Accounts of the number of deaths diverge widely between contemporary newspaper reports and later studies and testimonies, with no definitive figure.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsEl Telégrafo (1922-11-17) report: 9 dead, 76 injured (original government official tally unconfirmed)Testimony of Alfredo Pareja Diezcanseco: 250–350 / Historian William F. Varela: Review of death certificates found over 300, not exceeding 400 / Some historians: 300–500 / Survivor testimonies up to 2,000 (Varela considered this unsupported by evidence)

Data Record: This is a protest suppression event that does not meet the criteria of armed conflict datasets such as COW and UCDP (combat deaths), and therefore does not appear in the modern data table generated by the generator.

  1. Primicias, site of the 1922 November 15 massacre of workers in Guayaquil and historical controversy · Major media (Ecuador) · Orientation: Parallel presentation of figures from multiple historians and testimonies, without adjudicating specific figures — https://www.primicias.ec/guayaquil/lugares-masacre-obrera-15noviembre-centro-guayaquil-controversia-historica-109287/
  2. Enrique Ayala Mora, 'Héroes anónimos' (contribution to Primicias) · Historian's contribution to the press · Orientation: Commemorative perspective on victims of the labor movement — https://www.primicias.ec/noticias/firmas/heroes-anonimos-masacre-15noviembre-guayaquil

National strike in October 2019 (against the abolition of fuel subsidies) Protest · 2019

From October 2–13, 2019, nationwide protests led by indigenous movements continued. The Inter-American Commission on Human Rights (CIDH) conducted a field mission from October 28 to 30, interviewing 439 people and recording 380 testimonies of human rights violations, and recommended an integrated compensation plan for victims. The number of casualties differs between the national human rights institution (Defensoría del Pueblo) and government announcements, and the Defensoría's figures have also been noted to change depending on the time point.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
FatalitiesGovernment announcement: 6 (cited in reports; original government text unverified)Defensoría del Pueblo: approx. 10
InjuriesGovernment: approx. 1,500 treated in the public healthcare system (including 435 police officers; cited in reports)Defensoría del Pueblo: 1,340
DetaineesGovernment: approx. 1,400 (cited in reports)Defensoría del Pueblo: 1,192

Data Record: Not a conflict between states, so it does not meet the COW or UCDP civil war criteria; protest and suppression events must be verified from ACLED and MM-affiliated data.

  1. Vistazo, 'CIDH recomienda a Ecuador reparación para víctimas de protestas' (2020-01-16) · Major media (Ecuador), citing figures from international organizations and national human rights institutions · Orientation: Reporting centered on human rights institution figures — https://www.vistazo.com/actualidad/nacional/cidh-recomienda-ecuador-reparacion-para-victimas-de-protestas-BYVI164511

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The 1932 Western Peasant and Indigenous Uprising and the 'Matanza'Uprising · 1932

According to the Latin American History entry in Encyclopedia.com, after the military government took power following a coup on December 2, 1931 that ousted Arturo Araujo's elected government, an uprising began in the early hours of January 22, 1932 in the western suburbs of the capital (Ahuachapán, Santa Ana, and Sonsonate) and near Lake Ilopango to the east. Communist Party Secretary General Agustín Farabundo Martí was arrested four days before the uprising; he was subsequently executed in the capital after a formal trial alongside his comrades Alfonso Luna and Mario Zaldivar. The military government led by General Maximiliano Hernández Martínez directed the suppression, and the large-scale killings during the suppression are known as the 'Matanza.' Estimates of the number of fatalities vary widely across sources.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Fatalities in Suppression ProcessGovernment official tally not confirmed (account of the then-government: Méndez, 1932 — unread)Estimated range of 3,000 to 30,000; approximately 10,000 is a reasonable estimate (author of the Encyclopedia.com entry); COW combat deaths: 2,600 (see dataset table)

Data Record: COW Intra-State Wars v5.1 records 2,600 combat deaths in the 'Matanza War of 1932' — this indicator does not include non-combat killings over the subsequent weeks after the uprising, so its academic estimates (thousands to tens of thousands) differ in unit from those of the academic estimate.

  1. 'Matanza', Encyclopedia of Latin American History and Culture (Encyclopedia.com) · Academic encyclopedia entry · Tone: Anglo-American academic narrative; victim estimates include author's judgment — https://www.encyclopedia.com/humanities/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/matanza
  2. J.H.H. van den Berk, The Middle Men: The American Foreign Service and the Dictators of Central America, 1930-1952 (Ph.D. thesis, Leiden University, 2012) · Academic monograph — background on the Salvadoran military regime in the 1930s · Tone: U.S. diplomatic document-centered perspective; victim figures not verified in this round of text — https://scholarlypublications.universiteitleiden.nl/access/item%3A2960853/view

The Salvadoran Civil War (1980–1992) and the UN Truth Commission Report Civil War · 1979–1992

According to Human Rights Watch (1994 Annual Report), the UN Truth Commission released its report From Madness to Hope: The 12-Year War in El Salvador in mid-March 1993. The report covered killings, disappearances, and massacres attributed to government forces and death squads alongside killings and abductions attributed to the FMLN, and attributed approximately 85% of cases submitted to the commission to state agents, paramilitary organizations, and death squads linked to the state, and approximately 5% to the FMLN. It named more than 40 military officers and 11 FMLN members by name and recommended a decade-long ban from public office for them; it also stated that Defense Minister René Emilio Ponce had ordered the killings of six Jesuit priests, their housekeeper, and her daughter in 1989. The assassinations of Archbishop Romero in 1980 and the El Mozote massacre in 1981 were also covered in detail. A few days after the report was released, President Cristiani requested an 'immediate, general, and blanket amnesty,' and the Legislative Assembly passed a comprehensive amnesty law amid opposition from the FMLN and the opposition. The government describes the amnesty as a reconciliation measure, while human rights organizations describe it as institutionalized impunity.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Attribution of Perpetrators in Truth Commission CasesUN Truth Commission (1993): State agents and linked organizations approx. 85%, FMLN approx. 5%HRW (1994) directly cites the commission's figures — independent tally could not be verified separately
War DeathsOfficial government tally unconfirmedCOW combat deaths: 24,000 (see data set table); commonly cited figure of approx. 75,000 unconfirmed in this round

Data record: COW records the 1979–1992 civil war (combat deaths 24,000), while UCDP records conflict between the government and ERP, FPL, and FMLN. Truth commission cases focus on non-combatant civilian harm, so their units differ from combat death indicators.

  1. Human Rights Watch, World Report 1994 — El Salvador · Human rights organization report · Orientation: human rights violations and impunity critique perspective — https://www.hrw.org/reports/1994/WR94/Americas-04.htm

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

1526 Indigenous Uprising under Governor Salcedo Spanish Conquest · 1526

According to the Library of Congress Country Study (Honduras: A Country Study), Governor Diego López de Salcedo's policies in 1526 drove many indigenous peoples who had been 'pacified' during Cortés's expedition into open rebellion. The same source notes that in the early 1530s, rebellions, internal strife among Spaniards, disease, and slave exports combined to bring the colony to the brink of collapse. Dates and magnitudes of individual uprisings do not appear in this source.

  1. Library of Congress Federal Research Division, Honduras: A Country Study — 'The Spanish Conquest' · Government research institute country study (U.S. Library of Congress) · Orientation: secondary narrative based on Spanish chronicles, limited indigenous sources — https://countrystudies.us/honduras/8.htm

1537–1539 Lempira's Lenca Uprising (Fort Serkin) Spanish Conquest · 1537–1539

According to the same country study, in 1537 a young Lenca leader named Lempira led an uprising. Using a fortified hill called 'Peñol de Serkin' as his base, he repelled all Spanish attempts to suppress the rebellion until 1538; thereafter other indigenous groups rose up as well, threatening the entire region of Jalapa. Lempira was killed during negotiations with the Spanish, and resistance subsequently collapsed, though some fighting continued into 1539. The same source notes that approximately 15,000 indigenous people remained under Spanish control in 1539 and approximately 8,000 in 1541. The scale of participation in the uprising (approx. 30,000 people, over 200 villages) is a figure from the chronicle writer Herrera in the 1620s; the original text could not be accessed in this round. Honduras subsequently named its currency the 'Lempira' to commemorate him.

  1. Library of Congress Federal Research Division, Honduras: A Country Study — 'The Spanish Conquest' · Government research institute country study (U.S. Library of Congress) · Orientation: Secondary account based on the Spanish chronicle — https://countrystudies.us/honduras/8.htm

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

1954 General Strike of Banana Workers Protest · 1954

According to the Swarthmore College World Nonviolent Action Database, the strike began in April 1954 when dock workers protested over holiday pay and continued until July 9. By around May 5, banana industry strikers numbered about 25,000, approaching 15% of Honduras's workforce; by the second week of May, some 11,000 Standard Fruit workers joined in; and by around May 21, strikers numbered about 100,000. Tobacco, brewery, textile, and mining workers went on solidarity strikes alongside students, teachers, and artisans. Demands included a 72% wage increase, improved working conditions, and medical care for workers' families; as a result, wages rose by 21%, the companies agreed to family medical care and condition improvements, and the government proceeded with labor legislation. The same source notes that thousands were subsequently fired due to loopholes in the contracts, leaving whether or not there was repressive violence 'unknown.' Other accounts (labor history studies) report shootings by military and company guards and arrests of leadership, but the original texts could not be accessed in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Number of ParticipantsHonduran government official tally; original text unconfirmedApprox. 25,000 (May 5) → approx. 100,000 (May 21) — Global Nonviolent Action Database
Wage IncreasesCompany and government announcements; original texts unconfirmedDemanded 72%, settled at 21% — Global Nonviolent Action Database

Data Record: This strike does not appear as a separate campaign in the NAVCO and Mass Mobilization (post-1990) lists. Dataset entries follow the generator table.

  1. Global Nonviolent Action Database (Swarthmore College), 'United Fruit Company laborers campaign for economic justice in Honduras, 1954' · Academic database at a university · Orientation: Nonviolent movement research perspective, centered on workers' outcomes — https://nvdatabase.swarthmore.edu/content/united-fruit-company-laborers-campaign-economic-justice-honduras-1954

June 28, 2009 Coup and Subsequent Crackdowns on Protests Coup · 2009

According to the Honduras section of Human Rights Watch's 2012 World Report on Human Rights, following the June 2009 coup, the de facto government suspended civil liberties such as freedom of the press and assembly, and the military occupied opposition-leaning media outlets, temporarily interrupting broadcasts. Police and military forces responded with excessive force to largely peaceful protests, resulting in multiple deaths, numerous injuries, and thousands of arbitrary detentions. The Truth Commission established by the Porfirio Lobo government in May 2010 recorded 20 deaths in its July 2011 report — concluding that 12 died from excessive use of force by police and military personnel, while 8 were killed by intentional shootings by state agents, and noting that police and military had 'systematically obstructed' the investigation by tampering with crime scenes and official documents. The ousted president was Manuel Zelaya. Those who supported the coup (the then Congress and Supreme Court) argued that Zelaya's push for a constitutional amendment referendum was unconstitutional and constituted a legal dismissal (the original text of this claim could not be accessed in this round). As of October 2011, according to the Human Rights Special Prosecutor's Office, no one had been criminally convicted for these abuses.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Truth Commission Recorded Deaths20 (excessive force: 12, intentional killings: 8) — Government-established Truth Commission July 2011 report (cited by HRW)'Several deaths, thousands of arbitrary detentions' — HRW World Report on Human Rights 2012
Criminal Convictions0 cases (October 2011, Human Rights Special Prosecutor's Office)HRW 2012 identical citation

Data Record: Powell & Thyne records it as 'success' on 2009-06-28; Archigos records Zelaya's ouster on the same day; NAVCO records it as the FNRP (2009) campaign. UCDP does not appear because it falls short of its armed conflict criteria.

  1. Human Rights Watch, 'Informe Mundial 2012: Honduras' · Report by an international human rights organization · Orientation: Centered on accounts of human rights violations — https://www.hrw.org/es/world-report/2012/country-chapters/259689

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Revolts

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

The Artigas Revolution of 1811 and the Battle of Las Piedras and the Great Escape 1811–1815

According to Uruguay: A Country Study by the Library of Congress Federal Research Division, José Gervasio Artigas's army achieved its most important victory against Spanish forces at Las Piedras on May 18, 1811, and laid siege to Montevideo from May to October. Following the peace treaty in October 1811, Artigas led a force of approximately 3,000 soldiers and 13,000 civilians out of Salto across the Uruguay River to Ayuí in Argentina for several months; the same source describes this movement as the first step in the formation of the Uruguayan nation. After parting ways with Buenos Aires in 1813, sieges continued, and after the withdrawal of the Buenos Aires forces in 1814, Artigas's forces took Montevideo early in 1815.

  1. Library of Congress Federal Research Division, Uruguay: A Country Study — 'Artigas's Revolution, 1811-20' · Government research institute country study (U.S. Library of Congress) · Orientation: English-language secondary accounts from around 1990, centered on independent narratives — http://countrystudies.us/uruguay/3.htm

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Blanco's (National Party) Uprising of 1897 in Saravia Uprising · 1897

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, in 1897 a Blanco force led by Aparicio Saravia rose up in arms over discontent that only three of the four states promised under the 1872 treaty had gone to the National Party (Blanco). The uprising put forward guarantees of elections, secret ballots, and proportional representation. President Bordaberry was assassinated before a military resolution was reached, and his successor Cuestas negotiated an accord with the National Party under which the party took six states and all citizens' political rights were promised. The number of fatalities is not given in this source.

Data record: The COW civil war data includes only the 1904 war, and the 1897 uprising does not appear in the data file.

  1. Library of Congress Federal Research Division, Uruguay: A Country Study — 'Modern Uruguay, 1875-1903' · Government research agency country study (U.S. Library of Congress) · Orientation: English-language secondary account — http://countrystudies.us/uruguay/11.htm

1972 Tupamaros armed struggle and declaration of 'state of internal war' Civil War · 1963–1972

According to the Library of Congress Federal Research Division's country study, after a six-month electoral ceasefire from October 1971, the Tupamaros resumed armed activity in April 1972; following bloody gun battles, President Bordaberry declared a 'state of internal war.' Civil liberties were suspended for the first 30 days and then extended by the General Assembly (Congress) until 1973, and the Law on National Security was enacted on July 10, 1972. The same source notes that by late 1972 the military had decisively defeated the Tupamaros, with surviving members either imprisoned or exiled. The Tupamaros have described themselves as an armed national liberation movement, while the government and military have portrayed them as a terrorist organization. This source does not give figures for fatalities or detainees.

Data record: UCDP/PRIO records only one year of fighting against MLN/Tupamaros in 1972 (fewer than 1,000 combat deaths); the late-1960s activity appears below the UCDP threshold and does not appear in the data.

  1. Library of Congress Federal Research Division, Uruguay: A Country Study — 'The Emergence of Militarism, 1972-73' · Government research agency country study (U.S. Library of Congress) · Orientation: English-language secondary account, some Cold War perspective — http://countrystudies.us/uruguay/21.htm

Dissolution of Congress (self-coup) on June 27, 1973, and CNT general strike; ouster of Bordaberry in 1976 Coup · 1973–1985

According to the Library of Congress Federal Research Division's country study, on June 27, 1973, President Bordaberry, with military support, dissolved the General Assembly and replaced it with a National Council. The National Labor Council (CNT) called for factory occupations and then staged a general strike that lasted nearly two weeks. The same source notes that in 1976 Amnesty International estimated Uruguay to have 'the highest number of political prisoners per capita in the world.' In June 1976, after proposing the abolition of parties and a permanent dictatorship with himself as president, Bordaberry was forced to resign. In a 1980 referendum, voters rejected the military's proposed constitution by 57 to 43. Figures for disappeared persons are published by Uruguayan government bodies (the Office for Human Rights of Recent and Recent Past under the Presidency), but in this round the original government text could not be accessed, so figures are not recorded (unverified reference).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
1980 Military Constitutional Amendment ReferendumOpposition 57% to 43% in favor (result reported by the Library of Congress Country Study)Independent tally unconfirmed for this round

Data record: The Powell & Thyne coup data records coups on 1973-02-10 and 1976-06-12 as successful, without a separate entry for the dissolution of Congress on June 27, 1973. The country study describes the June 27 event as a key event — difference in definition.

  1. Library of Congress Federal Research Division, Uruguay: A Country Study — 'The New Situation, 1973-80' · Government research agency country study (U.S. Library of Congress) · Orientation: English-language secondary account — http://countrystudies.us/uruguay/22.htm

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Tacky's War 1760–1761

In the vicinity of St. Mary Parish around Easter 1760, an uprising led by Akkan (Coromantee) slave leader Takyi broke out and subsequently spread west across the island. British forces and allied contingents attacked the rebels within a week, killing Takyi; some of the remaining rebels are said to have committed mass suicide in caves near what is today known as 'Tacky Falls'. A Jamaican Gleaner article describes approximately 60 British deaths and hundreds of rebel fatalities, with hundreds more executed and around 300 deported to Honduras for logwood harvesting. The number of rebel fatalities varies across sources between 'hundreds' and '300–400'.

  1. Chief Takyi, folk hero Tacky - The Gleaner (2022-04-13) · Media · Lean: Jamaican major daily, commemorative article — https://past.jamaica-gleaner.com/article/news/20220413/chief-takyi-folk-hero-tacky
  2. Tacky's War (1760-1761) - BlackPast.org · Public History DB · Lean: African-centered historical narrative — https://blackpast.org/global-african-history/tackys-war-1760-1761

5. Modern Events through the Literature

Sam Sharpe Rebellion (Baptist War · Christmas Rebellion) Uprising · 1831–1832

Baptist lay deacon Samuel ('Sam') Sharpe planned a labor strike (passive strike) after Christmas in 1831, which escalated into an armed uprising around Montego Bay on December 27, 1831. The British navy and militia suppressed the rebellion; Sharpe was publicly hanged in Montego Bay on May 23, 1832. This event is cited as a backdrop to discussions of abolition in the British Parliament. Reported fatalities vary across sources.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths in the First Week of Suppression (Rebel Side)Approximately 200 (Royal Museums Greenwich collection description)
White (European) Deaths14 (first week, Royal Museums Greenwich) / 16 (Moon Jamaica)
ExecutionsOver 300 (Moon Jamaica)

Data Record: Events from 1816 onward; no entries for Jamaica in modern datasets such as COW, UCDP, or NAVCO due to the colonial period.

  1. Sam Sharpe - National Library of Jamaica (Labour Day 2010) · Government (National Library) · Lean: Commemorative narrative of a national hero — https://nlj.gov.jm/labourday/samsharpe.html
  2. Attack and Capture of the Rebels positions near Montego Bay, 1831 - Royal Museums Greenwich · Museum collection description · Lean: British national museum, account of suppression — https://rmg.co.uk/collections/objects/rmgc-object-241921
  3. Sam Sharpe, National Hero - Moon Jamaica · Guidebook · Orientation: popular narrative, not academic verification — https://www.moonjamaica.com/node/1862

Morant Bay Rebellion Uprising · 1865

On October 11, 1865, a group led by Baptist deacon Paul Bogle marched to the Morant Bay courthouse in the St. Thomas parish, and clashes spread after militia firing. The uprising was suppressed within about a week, but military reprisals continued for several weeks under martial law proclaimed by Governor Eyre. Bogle was hanged on October 24, 1865 (Jamaica Information Service JIS). Subsequently, controversy erupted in Britain over Eyre's enforcement of martial law. Figures for deaths, executions, floggings, and burned houses differ across sources.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths (including suppression and reprisals)439 (contemporary tally, cited by Cornhill Magazine — LRB review)Approx. 500 (JIS) / over 1,000 (Jamaica Observer)
Executions354over 400 (makingfreedom.co.uk)
Floggingsapprox. 600
Houses Burnedover 1,000 (makingfreedom.co.uk)

Data Record: As an event of the colonial era, Jamaica has no entries in modern datasets such as COW or UCDP.

  1. Paul Bogle - Jamaica Information Service · Government · Orientation: National Hero commemorative narrative — https://jis.gov.jm/information/heroes/paul-bogle/
  2. Stephen Sedley, 'No Law at All' - London Review of Books 28(21) · Academic review · Orientation: British jurist's perspective on martial law — https://www.lrb.co.uk/v28/n21/stephen-sedley/no-law-at-all
  3. Morant Bay Rebellion – 1865 - Making Freedom · Educational material · Orientation: Project for teaching slavery and resistance history — https://makingfreedom.co.uk/morant-bay-rebellion-1865

Operation to Enter Tivoli Gardens, West Kingston Protest · 2010

In May 2010, the Jamaican police (JCF) and military (JDF) entered Tivoli Gardens in West Kingston to arrest Christopher Coke, who was subject to a U.S. extradition request; multiple civilians died amid clashes with armed forces. The West Kingston Commission of Inquiry (Simmons Commission) report was released on June 15, 2016, pointing to excessive use of force by security forces and weaknesses in command chains. Amnesty International recorded that the commission found evidence suggesting extrajudicial killings but no criminal prosecutions ensued. Residents have long claimed over 100 deaths.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Civilian Deaths69 (West Kingston Commission of Inquiry) / 74 (Public Defender interim report) / 76 (Public Defender follow-up report)Over 100 (resident claims, media reports)
Security Force Deaths1 soldier (+2 police in other Kingston areas)

Data Record: Not listed in UCDP armed conflict or COW civil war lists. MM protest data counts only 1 event for 2010; this operation was a security rather than protest operation and thus does not fit the dataset categories (classified under 'protest' for convenience of classification, but its nature is a state suppression operation).

  1. Amnesty International, Jamaica: Waiting in vain (AMR 38/4337/2016) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: victim and accountability perspective — https://www.amnesty.org/es/wp-content/uploads/2021/05/AMR3843372016ENGLISH.pdf
  2. Tivoli report didn't hold citizens culpable - The Gleaner (2016-06-17) · Media · Orientation: Major Jamaican daily — https://past.jamaica-gleaner.com/article/news/20160617/tivoli-report-didnt-hold-citizens-culpable-lawyer
  3. Tivoli death toll could have been much higher - Ellington, The Gleaner (2015-04-21) · Media · Orientation: Reporting testimony of a police commander — https://past.jamaica-gleaner.com/article/lead-stories/20150421/tivoli-death-toll-could-have-been-much-higher-ellington

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Battle of Curalava and the Great Mapuche Rebellion Modern Era (Colonial Period) · 1598–1603

The defeat of Spanish forces at Curalava in 1598 was a starting point for one of the largest Mapuche uprisings during the colonial period. Memoria Chilena, the national library of Chile, describes how this uprising destroyed Spanish cities south of the Bio-Bio River, and as a result, the Spanish population concentrated into a narrow area rather than dispersing over the first 60 years of conquest as had previously been the case. Up until then, defense had relied on contributions (deramas) from cabildos and residents, but in 1603 Governor Alonso de Rivera obtained an annual royal subsidy 'real situado' from the Crown and established a professional army for border defense. The same source notes that as the Mapuche adopted horses and firearms, warfare shifted to rapid and frequent raids. This account is a consolidation by a national institution based on Spanish administrative and military records and should be read alongside oral traditions of the Mapuche.

  1. Memoria Chilena (National Library of Chile) — Ejército español · Public History DB · Orientation: Chilean state institution, centered on records of Spanish colonial administration — https://www.memoriachilena.gob.cl/602/w3-article-92258.html

5. Modern Events through Literature

2019 Social Outburst (Estallido social) Protests · 2019–2020

From around October 17, 2019, large-scale protests continued across Chile. The National Institute for Human Rights (INDH), a state human rights body, released figures on injuries sustained during police crackdowns; as of the November report, it recorded 241 eye injuries (161 ocular trauma, 73 ocular damage, 5 eye loss, 2 eye rupture) and 8,165 detentions at police stations, and by the December 30 report, 359 eye injuries (trauma/damage 335, rupture/loss 24), 3,583 injured in total, of whom 2,050 were related to firearms (rubber bullets 1,615, tear gas 230, rubber bullets 185, live ammunition 51). In the same period, INDH filed 1,140 complaints and judicial proceedings numbered 980 (torture 777, sexual violence 137). The figures cannot be directly compared because the criteria for accumulation and classification varied at each reporting point. Official government and police tallies were not confirmed in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
Eye Injuries (as of 2019.11.30)INDH 241Unconfirmed
Eye Injuries (as of 2019.12.30)INDH 359Unconfirmed
Injured (as of 2019.12.30)INDH 3,583Unconfirmed

Data Record: INDH is a state institution (autonomous human rights body), so it is placed in the 'Official' column. Police and Ministry of Interior tallies and independent investigations (Amnesty International, etc.) could not be verified because the original texts were not accessed in this round. The corresponding year records in the NAVCO and ACLED tables are populated by the generator from data/CHL.json.

  1. CNN Chile — INDH Report: 359 eye injuries, etc. (2019.12.31) · Major Media · Orientation: Chilean private broadcaster, relaying INDH announcement — https://www.cnnchile.com/pais/informe-indh-lesionados-heridas-oculares-estallido-social_20191231/
  2. Emol — INDH reports 241 eye injuries (2019.11.30) · Major Media · Orientation: Chilean conservative daily (El Mercurio group), relaying INDH announcement — https://www.emol.com/noticias/Nacional/2019/11/30/968921/INDH-ocular-reporte-crisis-estallido.html

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

1948 Costa Rican Civil War Civil War · 1948

According to the International War Memorial Database (war-memorial.net), which reproduces PRIO battle deaths data, opposition candidate Otilio Ulate won the presidential election in February 1948, but when the government-majority legislature declared the election results invalid, a rebel force led by José Figueres rose up against the government of President Teodoro Picado. The same source records the combat period as lasting from March 12 to April 24, 1948, for 44 days. The government side cited electoral fraud as the reason for invalidation, while the rebels viewed the invalidation itself as a rejection of the election results. The material linked in this round does not adjudicate either side's claims, nor does this narrative.

ItemOfficial FiguresIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmed by official Costa Rican government figuresApprox. 2,000 (war-memorial.net), PRIO battle deaths data v3.0 lower bound 1,000 · upper bound 2,000; COW 1,600 (see data table)
Combat PeriodUnconfirmed1948-03-12 ~ 1948-04-24, 44 days (war-memorial.net)

Data Record: Recorded in both COW (war number 730, combat deaths 1,600) and UCDP (conflict ID 225, government vs. guerrilla). Death estimates range from COW's 1,600 to PRIO's 1,000–2,000, showing a gap between datasets. The coup data (Powell & Thyne) contain no entry for the 1948 event — it appears classified as an armed uprising rather than fitting the definition of a coup.

  1. Costa Rican Civil War 1948 — a memorial for all wars (war-memorial.net), citing PRIO Battle Deaths v3.0 · Private war memorial database (academic dataset republishing) · Orientation: secondary aggregation, concise narrative — https://war-memorial.net/Costa-Rican-Civil-War-3.87
  2. Del figuerismo como historia — La Nación (2010-12-08) · Major media (Costa Rican daily newspaper) op-ed · Orientation: an op-ed by a critic of the Figueres faction — https://www.nacion.com/archivo/del-figuerismo-como-historia/ZJZ4EKONYFDBPMSBNLKQFAAXOQ/story/

Rule of the National Constituent Assembly (Second Republic military government) 1948–1949 Overthrow of Government · 1948–1949

An op-ed published in the Costa Rican daily La Nación (2010-12-08) notes that after the presidential election on February 8, 1948, the Figueres faction rose up armed on March 12, and on March 31, Calderón Guardia and Ulate agreed to give the presidency to Julio César Ovares for two years. The de facto government that took power after the civil war (the National Constituent Assembly) suspended the 1871 Constitution and established a special court; Ulate took office as president after waiting 18 months. The same op-ed notes that this government brought about institutional changes, including women's suffrage, the establishment of the Supreme Electoral Tribunal, and the abolition of the army, while also recording selective killings of Calderón supporters and communists, such as the Codo del Diablo case. While the author views the Figueres faction as having used the invalidation of the election as a pretext for the uprising, the narrative of the Figueres faction regards the invalidation as a rejection of the election results (see preceding item). This narrative adjudicates neither side.

ItemOfficial FiguresIndependent Investigations & Other
Military Government PeriodUnconfirmed18 months until Ulate took office — La Nación op-ed
Victims of Selective Killings (e.g., Codo del Diablo)UnconfirmedCase mentioned, no figures — La Nación op-ed

Data Record: Archigos may record the 1948–1949 leadership change as irregular (see data table). Not recorded in coup data.

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Communeros Uprising 1781

The uprising began on March 16, 1781, during the fair in El Socorro (now in Santander Department). Under financial pressure from the war with Britain, the Spanish royal court imposed new taxes through the intendant Francisco Gutiérrez de Piñeres, and combined with a decline in gold mining, discontent erupted among various classes including criollos, mestizos, indigenous peoples, and slaves. Popular tradition holds that Manuela Beltrán tearing up the tax proclamation was the trigger, but historians do not agree on her role. The uprising forces reached Zipaquira, and on June 8, Juan Francisco Berbeo signed the 'Zipaquira Pact' proposed by Archbishop Caballero de Góngora, recognizing the rebels' demands, though the colonial government soon invalidated it. José Antonio Galán, who led the second phase of the uprising, was arrested on October 13, sentenced to death in January 1782, and executed; his remains were displayed in Guadual, Socorro, San Hil, Chalala, and Mogotes. Later historiography includes interpretations viewing this uprising as a precursor to independence.

  1. Radio Nacional de Colombia, 'Historia del levantamiento de los Comuneros' · Public broadcaster (Colombian state-owned) · Orientation: State public media — Views that regard it as a precursor of the independence narrative may be reflected — https://radionacional.co/cultura/historia-colombiana/independencia-de-colombia-historia-del-levantamiento-de-los-comuneros

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The Bogotazo — The Assassination of Jorge Eliécer Gaitán and the Capital Uprising Uprising · 1948

On April 9, 1948, Liberal Party leader Jorge Eliécer Gaitán was shot and killed by Juan Roa Sierra as he stepped out of his law office in downtown Bogotá; Roa Sierra was later killed by the crowd amid ensuing unrest. The riot that began in the capital spread nationwide, and historian David Bushnell described it as an explosion that engulfed nearly the entire country. The motives behind the assassination remain fully unexplained. This event is subsequently described in connection with the 'La Violencia' period. The 2011 Victims Law designated April 9 as 'Day of Remembrance and Solidarity for Victims'.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsApproximately 550 according to contemporary press reports (original government official tally unconfirmed)Some researchers: over 2,000

Data Record: The COW civil war data records the 'First Wave of La Violencia (1948-1953)' as a single entry; the number of deaths in the Bogotazo event does not appear separately.

  1. El Universal (Cartagena), 'Asesinato de Gaitán, 70 años del crimen que partió en dos la historia colombiana' (2018-04-07) · Major Media (Colombia) · Orientation: Reports both contemporary press figures and researcher estimates without adjudication — https://www.eluniversal.com.co/colombia/2018/04/07/asesinato-de-gaitan-70-anos-del-crimen-que-partio-en-dos-la-historia-colombiana/

Guerrilla and Semi-Military Organization Armed Conflict with the State (1958–2012, Historical Memory Center tally) Civil War · 1958-2012

The Historical Memory Group of the National Center for Historical Memory (CNMH), a Colombian state institution, estimated in its 2013 report ¡Basta ya! that approximately 220,000 people died in armed conflict from January 1, 1958 to December 31, 2012. According to press reports, about 80% of the fatalities were civilians, and among 1,982 massacres over the past 30 years, the perpetrators were semi-military organizations (59%), guerrillas (17%), and state institutions (8%). The center also noted undercounting.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths (1958-2012)CNMH ¡Basta ya! (2013) approx. 220,000, of which approx. 176,000 were civilians (based on press reports)UCDP and COW maintain separate tallies based on combat deaths (see generator table)
Perpetrators of Massacres (1,982 cases)Semi-military organizations 59% / Guerrillas 17% / State institutions 8% (CNMH)Original independent tally unverified

Data Record: COW records this as the 'Second Wave of La Violencia (1955-1962)' and the 'FARC/ELN War (1988-present)', leaving 1962–1988 blank in its civil war list. The CNMH tally includes civilian deaths, so it cannot be directly compared with datasets based on combat fatalities.

Truth Commission on Armed Conflict Final Report Tally (1985–2018) Civil War · 1985-2018

The Truth, Coexistence and Non-Repetition Commission (CEV), established under the 2016 peace accord between the government and FARC-EP, released its final report on June 28, 2022. In collaboration with the Special Jurisdiction for Peace (JEP) and the Human Rights Data Analysis Group (HRDAG), the commission integrated data to count 450,664 victims of conflict-related killings between 1985 and 2018, estimating that this figure could reach approximately 800,000 when undercounting is taken into account. Forced disappearances (1985–2016) were counted at 121,768 (estimated at approximately 210,000), and forced displacement (1985–2019) at 7,752,964.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Aphonte Conspiracy 1812

In 1812, authorities in Havana uncovered a large-scale plot to rebel against slavery. The central figure, José Antonio Aponte, was an African-Cuban freeman carpenter and artist; during the investigation, a 'picture book' was found in his home. It is reported that the book contained landscapes, biblical stories, Roman goddesses, Spanish kings, and black men depicted as warriors, emperors, and scribes, and Aponte had to give a detailed account of its contents at his trial. The book subsequently disappeared, leaving only the interrogation record for its content. Aponte was publicly executed immediately after his testimony, and his head was hanged in a busy intersection of Havana. Whether Aponte directly commanded plots outside Havana remains a subject of debate among scholars.

  1. Digital Aponte / Ada Ferrer Exhibition Introduction - Vanderbilt University CLACX · Academic Institution (University Research Institute) · Orientation: Exhibition and research introduction page; exact date of execution not recorded — https://as.vanderbilt.edu/clacx/?p=395

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The PIC Uprising and the 1912 Massacre Uprising · 1912

In the summer of 1912, news of an uprising by Partido Independiente de Color (PIC) forces led by Evaristo Estenoz reached Havana, and in June the Cuban Congress authorized 1 million pesos and the use of force to suppress it. In the course of suppression, large numbers of Afro-Cubans, mainly from the Oriente region, were massacred. Researchers describe this as a massacre ordered by the state, citing the vulnerability of the landed elite and U.S. imperial influence (Payne). There is a large discrepancy between official government tallies and researcher estimates of the death toll.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Belligerent DeathsOver 2,000 (Cuban official source in July, cited in Newberry seminar introduction)2,000–6,000 (Aline Helg estimate, 2012 centennial essay — search summary stage, original text not reviewed)

Data Record: COW Intra-State Wars v5.1 records 1,050 combat deaths for the 'Cuban Black Uprising of 1912'. This differs from both official and researcher figures (over 2,000), and because COW records combat deaths, civilian massacre deaths may be omitted.

  1. Samantha Payne (Harvard) seminar introduction - Newberry Library Scholarly Seminars · Academic institution (research library) · Orientation: academic presentation abstract, 'state-ordered massacre' perspective — https://www.newberry.org/calendar/samantha-payne-harvard-university

July 11 (11J) Nationwide Protests Protest · 2021

Nationwide protests broke out in Cuba on July 11–12, 2021. Human Rights Watch described the protests as overwhelmingly peaceful and later reported arbitrary detention and abuse cases during subsequent detention and prosecution. Cuban courts have finalized convictions for over 380 protesters and bystanders (including several children). A 36-year-old singer, Díaz Raudenis Laurencio Tejeda, died during the protests; HRW noted that it appeared to be at the hands of police. It is also noted that the Cuban government has characterized this as unrest instigated by external forces, but the total number of officially detained individuals could not be confirmed in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other

DeathsUnconfirmed1 (Díaz Raudenis Laurencio Tejeda, HRW 2022)

Because the total number of officially detained individuals has not been disclosed, comparisons between official and independent figures diverge between conviction counts and detention counts.

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The 1699 Arena Uprising (Arena Massacre) Spanish Colonial Period (Capuchin Mission) · 1699

According to a historical article in the Trinidad Guardian (2026), on December 1, 1699, rebels attacked the San Francisco de los Arenales mission near present-day San Raphael in eastern Trinidad. The article states that the attack killed Fathers Esteban de San Félix and Marcos de Vicque and Friar Ramón de Figueroa, and that a Spanish colonial official, Governor José de León y Ezpeleta, was ambushed and killed while traveling to the mission. It also reports the death of Father Juan de Moscoso Sotomayor, whose account is linked to the uprising and its aftermath. Spanish authorities retaliated with military expeditions against the accused communities, executing, imprisoning, and forcibly relocating their members. The article does not provide a total number of indigenous deaths. Conflicts over labor and construction obligations at the mission are cited as causes of the uprising, but surviving records are mostly Spanish and church documents that do not convey the rebels' own accounts.

  1. Folklore and Myths of T&T: The Haunting Tale of Arena Massacre — Trinidad and Tobago Guardian · Major media (local daily historical article) · Orientation: Folkloric and commemorative in nature, relying on Spanish and church records — https://guardian.co.tt/article/folklore-and-myths-of-tt-the-haunting-tale-of-arena-massacre-6.2.2600764.e67ad3051a

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

1937 Labor Uprising (Butler Oilfield Strike and Riot) Uprising · 1937

According to the commentary of the National Trust of Trinidad and Tobago, labor activist Tubal Uriah "Buzz" Butler was accused of inciting unrest and rioting with a speech to oilfield workers on May 9, 1937, and kept to a Port-of-Spain rally schedule on June 17 instead of appearing in court. On June 19, when authorities planned his arrest, clashes broke out at the Port-of-Spain rally and Constable Charlie King was killed; the strike then spread nationwide. The British warship HMS Ajax arrived with four naval infantry platoons on June 22, followed by HMS Exeter on June 23, and the commentary states that the mere presence of these forces effectively quelled the protests. Fourteen people died and dozens were injured nationwide. Butler turned himself in on September 27 and was sentenced to two years' imprisonment, but he remained imprisoned on Nelson Island for six years until the end of World War II. The British government established a committee (the Foster Commission) to investigate the causes of the unrest. June 19 became a public holiday called 'Labour Day' under the 1972 law.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsColonial authorities' and Foster Commission's original official tallies unverified14 — National Trust of T&T
InjuriesColonial authorities' original official tally unverified'Dozens' — National Trust of T&T

Data Record: As an event from the colonial era, there are no records in COW, UCDP, NAVCO, or MM.

  1. Tubal Uriah "Buzz" Butler: Man of the People — National Trust of Trinidad and Tobago · Commentary by a government-affiliated heritage institution · Orientation: National hero commemoration perspective — https://nationaltrust.tt/butler-man-of-the-people/

1970 Black Power Protests and the Tétier Barracks Mutiny Protest · 1970

According to Global Voices (2025), Black Power ideology spread among students, trade unions, and artists after independence in 1962, and when marches and strikes continued in April 1970, the government of Prime Minister Eric Williams declared a state of emergency and arrested 15 Black Power leaders. At the same time, a portion of the military led by Lieutenant Rex Lasalle, Rafeek Sha, and Michael Bage refused to suppress protesters and took 30 hostages at Tétier Barracks at the northwest tip of the island. The coast guard remained loyal to the government, and the mutineers were isolated because only one narrow road led to the barracks; they surrendered after 10 days. Lasalle negotiated general amnesty and promotion as conditions but was arrested on charges of mutiny and treason, sentenced to 15 years' imprisonment by a military court in March 1971, and released on appeal in July 1972 (served 27 months, including 9 months in solitary confinement). Sha testified that he did not return fire when the coast guard fired on the mutineers' convoy. Meanwhile, some accounts such as those in Trinidad Express view the mutiny as stemming from junior officers' dissatisfaction with command rather than being directly linked to the Black Power movement (see below under unverified items).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Black Power Leaders ArrestedGovernment announcement original text unverified15 — Global Voices (2025)
Tétier Barracks HostagesGovernment announcement original text unverified30 — Global Voices (2025)
Duration of the MutinyGovernment announcement original text unverified10 days — Global Voices (2025)

Data Record: The 1970 event is not recorded in Powell & Thyne's coup list, UCDP, COW, or NAVCO (the barracks mutiny was not classified as an attempt to seize power).

  1. Rex Lasalle, a leader of Trinidad & Tobago's 1970 army mutiny in support of the Black Power movement, has died — Global Voices (2025-02-27) · International citizen journalism outlet · Orientation: Obituary article; substantial weight given to the recollections of the mutiny leader — https://globalvoices.org/2025/02/27/rex-lasalle-a-leader-of-trinidad-tobagos-1970-army-mutiny-in-support-of-the-black-power-movement-has-died/

1990 Jamaat al-Muslimeen Coup Attempt Coup · 1990

According to the Global Voices (2020) summary, on July 27, 1990, members of Jamaat al-Muslimeen led by Yasin Abu Bakr stormed the Red House (the parliament building), the Port of Spain police headquarters, and the state broadcaster in an attempt to overthrow the elected government. Prime Minister Arthur N. R. Robinson and his cabinet were held hostage for six days; 24 people died and many more were injured. Jamaat surrendered on condition of a pardon, the organization was not prosecuted, and Jamaat claims that the Privy Council recognized the pardon. The government established an investigative commission in September 7, 2010 (chairman David Simmons), and its report was submitted to parliament in 2014. Bakr initially stated his willingness to testify but did not appear, limiting the report's comprehensiveness. The report recommended monetary compensation for hostages and victims' families and the designation of July 27 as a national day of remembrance; the article notes that it is unclear whether the compensation has been implemented. Bakr is cited as stating in his testimony that he 'put 113 lives on the line.'

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsOriginal figures from the commission report (2014); original text not verified24 — Global Voices (2020)
Hostage Detention PeriodOriginal commission report text not verified6 days — Global Voices (2020)
ParticipantsOriginal commission report text not verifiedBakr's testimony: 113 people besides himself — Cited by Global Voices (2020)

Data Record: UCDP/PRIO records 1990 as a domestic armed conflict between the government and Jamaat al-Muslimeen (government contested, 1 year). The Powell & Thyne coup list and Archigos abnormal transfers contain no record (an attempt by a non-state armed group that failed to change the regime). ACLED records hundreds of political violence events and fatalities per year since 2018, mostly appearing to be criminal gang-related violence requiring distinction from uprising events (see data table).

  1. It's been 30 years since the attempted coup in Trinidad & Tobago — Global Voices (2020-07-28) · International citizen journalism outlet · Orientation: primarily victim and government perspectives, with some Jamaat claims noted — https://globalvoices.org/2020/07/28/its-been-30-years-since-the-attempted-coup-in-trinidad-tobago/
