2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Bayano Shimaron Uprising (Bayano War) Spanish Colonial Period (mid-16th century) · 1550s

According to the historical commentary in La Prensa, a daily Panamanian newspaper, African people who escaped after a slave ship ran aground near Darién established free slave communities (palenques) in the jungle, and Bayano led hundreds of Cimarrones (runaway slaves). The article introduces this as the largest Cimarrón uprising at the time and calls the period the 'Bayano War.' Spanish colonial authorities failed to break him, and Pedro de Ursúa, sent by the Viceroy of Peru, proposed a peace agreement dividing Panama into Spanish territory and Bayano's domain and guaranteeing freedom for his people. According to the article, Bayano accepted it but was betrayed when he went to sign the agreement and was arrested along with some of his subordinates; he was not executed but traded his subordinates' freedom for passage through Peru to Spain, where he spent the rest of his life. The article does not provide exact years or casualty figures, and most remaining records are Spanish documents.

  1. La historia de Bayano — La Prensa (Panamá) · Major Media (Local Daily Historical Commentary) · Tone: Commemorative and popular explanation, relying on Spanish sources — https://www.prensa.com/multimedia/la-historia-de-bayano/

5. Modern Events through Literature

January 1964 'Day of the Martyrs' Incident (Flag-Raising)Protest · 1964

According to an AP report (2025), on January 9, 1964, students protested against the US authorities in the Canal Zone, which prevented them from raising the Panamanian flag at a secondary school in the zone. The protests spread into broader opposition to the US presence in Panama and US military intervened. The report states that police and US forces killed 21 protesters. Panama commemorates this day as Martyrs' Day. The report notes that historians view this event as a turning point leading to Jimmy Carter's signing of the canal transfer agreement (transfer of control on December 31, 1999). The number of US fatalities and the US government's official casualty count could not be verified in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Panamanian FatalitiesOriginal texts of official counts by Panamanian and US governments unverified21 (protesters killed by police and US forces) — AP

Data Record: There is no entry for this event in the UCDP armed conflict or COW civil war lists (the protest and unrest did not meet the criteria for an armed conflict).

  1. Panama marks Martyrs' Day as Trump threatens to retake control of Panama Canal — AP (KPRC Click2Houston reprint, 2025-01-09) · Major Media (wire service) · Orientation: Report in the context of current canal disputes; event background summarized at a high level — https://www.click2houston.com/news/world/2025/01/09/panama-marks-martyrs-day-as-trump-threatens-to-retake-control-of-panama-canal/

1989 US invasion of Panama ('Operation Just Cause') and Noriega regime collapse Regime Overthrow · 1989–1990

According to an AP report (2022), the US 'Operation Just Cause' operation, which began on December 20, 1989, lasted about a month and aimed to oust Manuel Noriega on drug and organized crime charges and bring him to the United States. Noriega fell from power and was tried in the United States. Official estimates put Panamanian military fatalities at approximately 300 and civilian fatalities at 214; US forces reported 23 US military deaths. Human rights organizations believe there may have been more Panamanian fatalities. A truth commission established years earlier documented about 20 disappearances related to this military action. Some of the victims are buried in a common grave with unknown burial locations, and reports state that over 100 people who died during the invasion are buried in a cemetery in Panama City, with excavations and DNA testing underway for identification. President Laurentino Cortizo signed a decree establishing December 20 as an annual national day of mourning. The US government described the operation's purpose as Noriega's arrest, while Panamanian victim groups have sought to clarify and remember the victims — both accounts are recorded without adjudication.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Panamanian FatalitiesApproximately 300 military personnel · 214 civilians — official estimates (cited in AP report)Human rights organizations: Claim there may have been more (specific figures unconfirmed in this round)
US Military Fatalities23 — US military announcement (cited in AP report)Independent tally unverified
MissingApproximately 20 cases — truth commission record (cited in AP report)Independent tally unverified

Data Record: UCDP records the 1989 Panama government–US government interstate conflict (id 368) and a 1989 mutiny by the Gerardi faction (id 367) as separate conflicts each lasting one year. The Powell & Thyne coup data's 1989 failed coup entry appears to be linked to the Gerardi mutiny, but is based on the generator table.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Communeros Revolution (Asunción settler uprising) Spanish colonial period (first half of the 18th century, under jurisdiction of the Viceroyalty of Peru) · 1721–1735

The abstract of Mercedes Avellaneda's article published in the Brazilian journal História Unisinos addresses the Paraguayan Comuneros Revolution of 1721–1735 as a case in which violence was mobilized as a means of change in colonial society, introducing an interpretation that calls it 'the first revolutionary movement in America.' The actors introduced by the abstract are the residents of Asunción (Asuncenos), colonial authorities, and Jesuit mission villages. The generally known course includes the visit of Judge José de Antequera y Castro of the Audiencia of Charcas to investigate Governor Diego de Los Reyes Valdiviesa in 1721, who ousted him and took the governorship for himself, followed by a two-stage phase led by Fernando de Mompó from 1730 onward; however, these details were not verified in the academic source text for this round (unverified reference). The abstract does not present the number of fatalities or the specific outcome of suppression.

  1. Avellaneda, M. (2007). El recurso de la violencia como mecanismo de cambio social en la sociedad del Paraguay colonial a principios del siglo XVIII. História Unisinos 11(2) · Academic article · Orientation: the designation as 'the first revolutionary movement' is an interpretive claim; focuses on settler motives — https://www.revistas.unisinos.br/index.php/historia/article/view/5887

5. Modern Events through the Lens of Literature

'March of Paraguay' (1999) (Assassination of Vice President Argüna and Shootout in Front of Congress) Protest · 1999

According to a report by the Paraguayan daily Última Hora, following the assassination of Vice President Luis María Argüna on March 23, 1999, protests demanding the resignation of President Raúl Cubas continued in front of Congress. On the night of March 26, gunfire was exchanged around buildings near Congress; reports stated that snipers were positioned at multiple points. The same article notes 7 protest deaths in the main text, while the headline and list include 8 (including one who died from injuries a year later), with over 700 wounded. President Cubas resigned amid this crisis and an impending impeachment trial. According to the article, 39 people were charged, but only one — Walter Gamarra, whose shooting of crowds was captured on video — was sentenced to 25 years in prison; all charges against Rino Oviedo, who was accused as the mastermind, were dropped by a court in 2007.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Protest DeathsOriginal government official tally unconfirmed for this round7 (Última Hora main text) / 8 (same article headline and list, including 1 who died from injuries a year later)
WoundedUnconfirmedOver 700 (Última Hora)
Charges & Convictions39 charged, 1 convicted (25 years in prison) — judicial outcomes reported by Última Hora—

Data Record: The UCDP armed conflict record centers on years of factional military clashes such as 1947, 1954, and 1989; the March 1999 events are recorded neither as an armed conflict nor a coup (Powell & Thyne) but as a protest/political violence incident.

  1. Sin pena ni gloria quedaron las ocho víctimas de aquel Marzo Paraguayo — Última Hora · Major media (local daily) · Orientation: Retrospective article from the perspective of victims, mixing death tolls of 7 and 8 within the same article — https://www.ultimahora.com/sin-pena-ni-gloria-quedaron-las-ocho-victimas-aquel-marzo-paraguayo-n977835

2012 Curuguaty Clash and President Lugo's Impeachment Regime Overthrow · 2012

According to Global Voices (2016), on June 15, 2012, farmers occupying land in Marinaquere, Curuguaty, clashed with police, resulting in the deaths of 11 farmers and 6 police officers, totaling 17. President Fernando Lugo stepped down approximately 7 days later following impeachment by Congress. The report notes that no police officers were charged, no one was charged for the deaths of the 11 farmers, and a full trial for the 11 farmers was ongoing at the time of the article. Both Congress and the government viewed the impeachment as a constitutional procedure, while Lugo's camp and some regional governments saw it as a de facto coup without an opportunity to defend themselves — this book does not adjudicate but records both accounts together. There is also a reference to an independent investigation preliminary report citing 7 police deaths, so the number of police fatalities varies across sources (unverified).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsOriginal official reports from the Paraguayan government and prosecution unconfirmed for this round17 (11 farmers · 6 police) — Global Voices
ChargesOnly farmers charged; no police charged (as of Global Voices 2016)—

Data Record: Because it was a change brought about by congressional impeachment, it is not included in Powell & Thyne's coup list (1954, 1955, 1989, 2000) or Archigos' records of abnormal transfers. The Curuguaty clash does not meet the UCDP armed conflict criteria (government vs. organized group, 25+ deaths per year).

  1. Four Years and an Impeached President Later, Paraguay's Curuguaty Massacre Is Still in the Shadows — Global Voices · International citizen media · Orientation: Perspective of farmers and human rights, with low coverage of government narrative — https://globalvoices.org/2016/05/31/four-years-and-an-impeached-president-later-paraguays-curuguaty-massacre-is-still-in-the-shadows/

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Rebellions

Juan Santos Atahuallpa Uprising after 1742 Late Viceroyalty of Peru · 1742–1750s

The historian Alberto Flores Galindo (writing in Amauta no. 197, 1978) notes that popular uprisings had continued for a long time in the Andes before the 1780 Túpac Amaru uprising, citing as an example the prolonged struggle of Juan Santos Atahuallpa, which began in Tarma and Gran Pajonal in 1742. The same article also includes the plots of Oruro and Huayrachiri and attacks on local officials (corregidores) under this category. The scale of the uprising, casualties, and Spanish records of suppression could not be verified from the original sources in this round.

  1. Alberto Flores Galindo, 'La revolución de Túpac Amaru', Amauta no.197 (Lima, 1978-11-03), Marxists Internet Archive edition · Historian's contribution (digitally republished) · Orientation: Marxist social history perspective — https://www.marxists.org/espanol/floresgalindo/1978/tupac-amaru.htm

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

1780–1781 Tupac Amaru II Uprising 1780–1781

According to Flores Galindo, the uprising began in 1780 in the Tinta region under the command of cacique José Gabriel Tupac Amaru. He estimates the death toll of this uprising at approximately 100,000 out of an estimated population of about 2 million at the time. This estimate is a description by one scholar, and no official Spanish tally was found in this round. The date of the capture and execution of the uprising leader (Cusco, 1781) remains unverified as the Britannica original text could not be accessed at 403.

  1. Alberto Flores Galindo, 'La revolución de Túpac Amaru', Amauta no.197 (1978) · Historian's contribution · Orientation: Social history narrative sympathetic to the uprising perspective — https://www.marxists.org/espanol/floresgalindo/1978/tupac-amaru.htm

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

1980–2000 Armed Conflict (Shining Path · MRTA vs. State) Civil War · 1980–2000

The conclusion section of the Peruvian Truth and Reconciliation Commission (CVR) final report states that the most plausible estimate of fatalities in the 1980–2000 armed conflict is 69,280. The same conclusion section attributes 54% of CVR-reported fatalities to the Communist Party of Peru-Shining Path (PCP-SL) and records that 75% of fatalities were native speakers of Quechua or another indigenous language. The proportion of responsibility attributed to state institutions could not be confirmed in this round's excerpt from the conclusion. Accounts by government and armed group sides, as well as affected communities, differ, and this book makes no judgment but transcribes CVR figures verbatim.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Fatalities (1980–2000)69,280 — CVR Final Report (2003, state-established commission) highest plausibility estimateCOW 'Shining Path War of 1982–1992' combat deaths: 12,708 (combat deaths only; different period)
PCP-SL Responsibility Share54% of CVR-reported fatalitiesIndependent figures not confirmed in this round
Indigenous Language Mother Tongue Rate75% of fatalities (CVR)Independent figures not confirmed in this round

Data Record: COW records 12,708 combat deaths for 1982–1992, which differs from CVR's 1980–2000 total casualty estimate (69,280) in both period and definition. UCDP groups government-vs.-ELN, MIR, MRTA, and Shining Path fighting in 1965–2010 into a single conflict (ID 292).

  1. Comisión de la Verdad y Reconciliación (Perú), Informe Final — Conclusiones generales (2003) · Final Report of the National Truth Commission · Affiliation: State-established commission; military and some parties disputed the conclusions — https://www.cverdad.org.pe/ifinal/conclusiones.php

Anti-Government Protests and Crackdowns, December 2022–February 2023 Protest · 2022–2023

According to Al Jazeera (May 26, 2023), the crisis began when President Pedro Castillo attempted to dissolve Congress and rule by decree ahead of a third impeachment vote on December 7, 2022, and was subsequently impeached, removed from office, and arrested. Amnesty International's report 'Lethal racism' tallied 49 protest deaths between December and February, and an examination of 52 fatalities and injuries in Ayacucho, Huancayo, Andahuaylas, and Chincheros concluded that 20 of them could correspond to extrajudicial executions. The same article reported Amnesty's analysis that lethal force was more frequent in areas with large indigenous populations. The government of Dina Boluarte described the protests as the work of 'extremist forces seeking disorder and chaos.'

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Protest Deaths (2022-12 ~ 2023-02)Government official tally not confirmed in this round (ombudsman tally unverified)49 protesters (Amnesty International, Al Jazeera reports)
Cases Potentially Constituting Extrajudicial ExecutionsGovernment described protests as acts of extremist forces20 out of 25 reviewed fatalities (Amnesty International)

Data Record: Not a conflict under UCDP or COW criteria; reflected in ACLED annual tallies of protests and political violence (see generator table).

  1. Al Jazeera, 'Amnesty report finds racial bias in Peru's protest crackdown' (2023-05-26) · Major international media outlet · Affiliation: Summary of Amnesty International report — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2023/5/26/amnesty-report-finds-racial-bias-in-perus-protest-crackdown
  2. Amnesty International, 'Peru: Lethal racism' (AMR 46/6761/2023) · International human rights organization report · Affiliation: Victim and human rights perspective; government rebuttals are presented separately — https://www.amnesty.org/en/documents/amr46/6761/2023/en/

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

January 1958 General Strike (Originating from the Taxi Dispute) Protest · 1958

According to a retrospective article in The Tribune (23 February 2009), the dispute began when white-owned travel agencies and independent taxi drivers clashed over the share of tourist transport at the new Nassau Airport. When the travel agencies refused the call-in system, the taxi union led by Clifford Darling sought broader support, and the Bahamas Labour Union led by Randol Fawkes declared a general strike. The strike began on January 13, 1958, and halted New Providence Island for about three weeks until it was withdrawn on January 30. The governor requested naval ships, and British troops were dispatched from Jamaica to reinforce 300 police officers. An inquiry commission was not permitted, and the establishment of a transport authority was promised. The same article notes that within three months a senior British minister visited Nassau demanding constitutional reforms; in October 1958, a Department of Labour and a statutory dispute resolution procedure were established by law; and in 1959, the abolition of company voting rights, the extension of suffrage to all men aged 21 and over, and the creation of four new seats (all won by the Progressive Liberal Party, PLP) followed.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Strike PeriodPublished figures could not be confirmed1958-01-13 ~ 1958-01-30, approximately 3 weeks (The Tribune retrospective article)
Reinforced TroopsPublished figures could not be confirmed300 police officers reinforced by British troops stationed in Jamaica (The Tribune)

Data Record: No Bahamas entry exists in any of the COW, UCDP, coup, Archigos, or NAVCO datasets. The MM (mass protest) tally is also empty. This general strike was a nonviolent labor action and does not meet the criteria for civil war or armed conflict.

  1. The Tribune (Nassau), 'Strike that stirred the nation' (2009-02-23) · Retrospective article in a major local daily · Orientation: Focused on recollections of labor movement figures and PLP affiliates — https://www.tribune242.com/news/2009/feb/23/strike-that-stirred-the-nation

April 27, 1965 'Black Tuesday' Parliamentary Protest Protest · 1965

According to a The Tribune article from April 27, 2014, on April 27, 1965, opposition leader Linden Pindling threw the Speaker's mace (a ceremonial staff) out of a window and said, 'This is a symbol of national authority, and the people are outside.' The article introduces this as an act of civil disobedience, reporting that Foreign Minister Fred Mitchell said the day pushed forward the path to majority rule on January 10, 1967. A The Tribune article from August 7, 2020, notes that the topic of debate in the House at the time was redistricting, and that hundreds of people gathered below the window where the mace fell onto Rawson Square. Accounts from the ruling United Bahamian Party (UBP) side at the time could not be confirmed in this cycle.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Crowd on Rawson SquarePublished figures could not be confirmedHundreds of people (The Tribune 2020)

Data Record: The MM (mass protest) dataset covers only from 1990 onward and thus does not include this event. No casualties were reported for the parliamentary protest inside the building.

  1. The Tribune (Nassau), 'Black Tuesday anniversary recalled' (2014-04-27) · Article in a major local daily · Orientation: Focused on quoting remarks by PLP government officials — https://www.tribune242.com/news/2014/apr/27/black-tuesday-anniversary-recalled
  2. The Tribune (Nassau), 'Tributes paid to Black Tuesday veteran Walkes' (2020-08-07) · local major daily obituary/memorial article · Tone: context of mourning by participants — https://www.tribune242.com/news/2020/aug/07/tributes-paid-to-black-tuesday-veteran-walkes

It does not provide prescriptions, moral evaluations, leader assessments, or predictions. Layer 6 is reserved exclusively for Cheonghae (淸海).
